<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove a uniform positive spectral gap for the pure antiferromagnetic spin-one Heisenberg chain on even periodic rings, establishing the positive even-periodic formulation of the spin-one Haldane conjecture.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For the Hamiltonians <a href="#eq:main-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-H">[eq:main-H]</a>, the ground state is unique for every even $L\ge60$, and $$\begin{aligned} \gamma_L&>\frac4{105}\log\frac{80}{79} &&\text{for every even }L\ge60,\label{eq:gap60}\\ \gamma_L&>\frac{\log20}{784} &&\text{for every even }L\ge2304.\label{eq:gap2304} \end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$\Delta_1\ge\frac{\log20}{784}>0.$$ The spectral conclusions also hold for any simultaneously similar spin-one realization satisfying the same commutator and Casimir normalizations.
  - Explanation: The pure bilinear spin-one chain has a unique ground state and a uniform positive gap on the stated even periodic rings. Simultaneous similarity preserves the spectral conclusion.
- **C2** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-representation)): Let $S^x,S^y,S^z\in M_3(\mathbb C)$ satisfy $$[S^\alpha,S^\beta] =i\sum_\gamma\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}S^\gamma, \qquad \sum_\alpha(S^\alpha)^2=2I.$$ They are simultaneously similar to the standard self-adjoint spin-one matrices. If the original matrices are self-adjoint, the similarity can be chosen unitary. The corresponding tensor-power similarity preserves the complete spectrum of every periodic Hamiltonian $H_n$.
  - Explanation: The commutator and Casimir assumptions determine the spin-one representation up to simultaneous similarity; self-adjoint realizations admit unitary equivalence.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-energy)): For every $n\ge4$ and every $g\in SO(3)$, $$\label{eq:energy-bounds} -\frac{3n}{2}I\le H_n(g)\le nI.$$
  - Explanation: The twisted nearest-neighbor Hamiltonians obey uniform operator energy bounds that control exponential approximations.
- **C4** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-transfer)): For each $b>0$ there are a Hilbert space $\mathcal K_b$, a compact self-adjoint operator $X_b$, and a unitary representation $g\mapsto U_g$ of $\mathcal G$ commuting with $X_b$, such that $$\label{eq:spatial-trace} Z_n(b,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X_b^nU_g) \qquad(n\ge4,\ g\in\mathcal G).$$ The operator $X_b$ and the representation commute with complex conjugation. Moreover $X_b$ is Hilbert–Schmidt, so $X_b^k$ is trace class for every integer $k\ge2$. If $\lambda_i(b)$ are its nonzero eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, then $$\label{eq:spatial-moments} \sum_i\|\lambda_i(b)\|^k<\infty\quad(k\ge2),\qquad Z_n(b)=\sum_i\lambda_i(b)^n\quad(n\ge4).$$
  - Explanation: A compact self-adjoint spatial operator represents twisted physical partition functions as trace moments. Its spatial eigenvalues need not be positive.
- **C5** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-sectors)): Fix $b>0$ and let $X_b,U_g$ be as in Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>. There are real eigenvalue lists, counted with multiplicity, whose integer moments $I_k,O_k,N_k$ converge absolutely for $k\ge2$, with $$\begin{aligned} Z_n(b)&=I_n+2O_n+3N_n,\label{eq:sector-total}\\ Z_n(b,P)&=I_n+2O_n-N_n,\label{eq:sector-pi}\\ Z_n(b,C)&=I_n-O_n\qquad(n\ge4).\label{eq:sector-cyclic} \end{aligned}$$ The $I$ list is the restriction to the joint invariant subspace of $D_2$ and $C$. The $O$ list occurs twice and the $N$ list three times in the full eigenvalue list of $X_b$. All three moments are nonnegative at even orders. If $J_n$ denotes the moment on the $D_2$-invariant subspace, then $$J_n=I_n+2O_n.$$
  - Explanation: Rotation symmetry separates spatial moments into repeated sector lists; even moments remain nonnegative without discarding physical states.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-purity)): Let $(w_i)$ be a finite or countable family of nonnegative numbers with $\sum_iw_i=1$. Its purity $P=\sum_iw_i^2$ lies in $(0,1]$. The normalized squares $w_i^2/P$ have purity $P'$ satisfying $$\label{eq:purity-square} 1-P'\le f(1-P),\qquad f(u)=\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)^2}\quad(0\le u<1).$$ The function $f$ is increasing. Consequently $$\label{eq:two-squarings} \begin{split} 0<S(n,b),T(n,b)&\le1,\\ 1-S(2n,b)&\le f(1-S(n,b)),\\ 1-T(n,2b)&\le f(1-T(n,b)). \end{split}$$
  - Explanation: Normalized squaring concentrates probability weights and bounds the resulting purity defect quadratically.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-rounded-update)): Suppose $n\ge4$ is even, $b>0$, and $p,q\in[0,1)$ satisfy $$1-S(n,b)\le p,\qquad 1-T(2n,b)\le q.$$ If $p_+,q_+\in[0,1)$ obey $$\label{eq:rounded-update} \begin{split} p_+&\ge f\bigl(1-(1-p)^2(1-q)\bigr),\\ q_+&\ge f\bigl(1-(1-q)^2(1-p_+)\bigr), \end{split}$$ then $$1-S(2n,2b)\le p_+,\qquad 1-T(4n,2b)\le q_+.$$
  - Explanation: Spatial and thermal concentration bounds propagate together under doubling, with upward rounding allowed in the specified order.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-bootstrap)): For every even $L\ge4$, let $H_L$ be a non-scalar finite-dimensional self-adjoint Hamiltonian. Suppose that $a\in\mathbb R$ and, for each $b>0$, a real finite or countable list $(\lambda_i(b))$ satisfy $$\sum_i\|\lambda_i(b)\|^4<\infty,\qquad Z_L(b):=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_L+aLI)}=\sum_i\|\lambda_i(b)\|^L \quad(L\ge4\text{ even}).$$ Define $S,T$ by <a href="#eq:purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:purities">[eq:purities]</a> and $G$ by <a href="#eq:bootstrap-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-coefficient">[eq:bootstrap-coefficient]</a>. Let $n_0\ge4$ be even, $b_0>0$, and let $u_0,C$ satisfy $$\label{eq:bootstrap-hypotheses} 0<u_0<\frac16,\qquad G(u_0)\le C,\qquad r:=Cu_0<1,\qquad C(1-3u_0)>3.$$ Suppose $$\label{eq:bootstrap-seeds} S(n_0,b_0)\ge1-u_0,\qquad T(2n_0,b_0)\ge1-u_0.$$ For $j\ge0$, set $$\label{eq:dyadic-parameters} n_j=2^j n_0,\qquad b_j=2^j b_0,\qquad u_j=C^{-1}r^{2^j}.$$ Then $$\label{eq:dyadic-purities} S(n_j,b_j)\ge1-u_j,\qquad T(2n_j,b_j)\ge1-u_j.$$ For every even $L\ge n_0$, the physical ground state is unique, and the gap above it satisfies $$\label{eq:bootstrap-gap} \gamma_L>\frac{\log(1/r)}{b_0}.$$
  - Explanation: Two initial purities and the scalar hypotheses yield ground-state uniqueness and a uniform gap throughout the larger even lengths.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-thermal-inputs)): For $a=700741/500000$, the following tables give centers for the shifted partition functions $Z_n(b,g)$ in units $10^{-6}$. The absolute errors are less than $10^{-5}$ at $b=21/2$ and less than $30\cdot10^{-6}$ at $b=49/4$. $$\begin{array}{c\|rrr} &\multicolumn{3}{c}{b=21/2}\\ n&g=1&g=P&g=C\\ \hline 6&8945303&346734&939987\\ 8&3741281&567167&984108\\ 10&2327547&721090&1002613 \end{array} \qquad \begin{array}{c\|rr} &\multicolumn{2}{c}{b=49/4}\\ n&g=1&g=P\\ \hline 4&124885379&65757\\ 5&2925&2960742\\ 6&12870614&286599\\ 7&54069&1920414\\ 8&4639283&510140\\ 9&195455&1514846\\ 10&2654823&675419\\ 11&377629&1314544\\ 12&1900547&787405 \end{array}$$
  - Explanation: The tables state rigorous finite thermal trace enclosures; the appendices supply integer recurrences and explicit error control.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cappedmass)): Let $(t_i)$ be a finite or countable family with $0\le t_i\le h$ and $\sum_i t_i\le m$, where $h>0$ and $m\ge0$. For $r\ge1$, put $$\label{eq:capped-function} \mathcal C(m,h,r)=q h^r+(m-qh)^r,\qquad q=\lfloor m/h\rfloor.$$ Then $\sum_i t_i^r\le\mathcal C(m,h,r)$. Without the individual cap, $\sum_i t_i^r\le m^r$.
  - Explanation: A cap and a total-mass bound control higher power sums through an extremal concentrated mass distribution.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-polynomial-filter)): Let $\epsilon\ge0$, $v>0$, and let $(\lambda_i)$ be a real eigenvalue list with moments $M_j=\sum_i\lambda_i^j$, and suppose $\|M_j-c_j\|\le\epsilon$ for the indices occurring in a polynomial $F(x)=\sum_j f_jx^j$. Assume these moments converge absolutely and $F\ge0$ on $\mathbb R$. If $$B=\sum_j f_jc_j+\epsilon\sum_j\|f_j\| \quad\hbox{and}\quad F(x)>B\ \hbox{for }\|x\|\ge v,$$ then every $\|\lambda_i\|<v$.
  - Explanation: A nonnegative polynomial and enclosed moments exclude eigenvalues beyond a strict modulus threshold.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-seed60)): At inverse temperature $b=105/4$, $$S(60,b)>\frac78,\qquad T(120,b)>\frac78.$$
  - Explanation: The first finite initialization supplies the spatial and physical purity bounds needed for the periodic gap bootstrap.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-seed2304)): At inverse temperature $b=784$, $$1-S(2304,b)\le\frac{84513}{10^7}<\frac1{100},\qquad 1-T(4608,b)\le\frac{27493}{5\cdot10^6}<\frac1{100}.$$
  - Explanation: Rounded coupled updates supply smaller purity defects for the stronger eventual periodic gap bound.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-boundary-inputs)): Let $X_b$ be the spatial transfer operator of Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>, with the shift $a=700741/500000$, and put $$\begin{gathered} b_0=49/4,\qquad b_1=49/2,\\ \ell=999/1000,\qquad U=100139/100000,\qquad V=872/1000,\\ m_J=1.0657205,\qquad m_N=.2783155. \end{gathered}$$ At $b=b_0$, the full eigenvalue list, counted with multiplicity, is the $D_2$-invariant list $J$ together with three identical lists $N$. Every eigenvalue in $J$ has modulus less than $U$, and the sum of the twelfth powers of all eigenvalues in $J$ is at most $m_J$. Every eigenvalue in each $N$ list has modulus less than $V$, and that list’s twelfth powers sum to at most $m_N$. There is an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\ell$. Moreover, $$1-S(72,b_1)\le\frac{151249}{2500000}.$$ For $n=72$ and $n=120$, one has $E_0(n)+na<0$; hence a physical ground Boltzmann factor in $Z_n(b)$ exceeds one for every $b>0$.
  - Explanation: The intermediate periodic sector estimates and variational inputs are exposed for the companion boundary analysis.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-cor-local-limit)): For the self-adjoint Hamiltonians <a href="#eq:main-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-H">[eq:main-H]</a>, the normalized ground states of the even periodic rings have a subsequence, as $L\to\infty$, converging on every local operator to a state $\omega$. Every such limit satisfies $$\label{eq:local-limit-gap} \omega(A^*[H,A])\ge\frac{\log20}{784} \bigl(\omega(A^*A)-\|\omega(A)\|^2\bigr)$$ for every local operator $A$.
  - Explanation: Subsequential local limits inherit a local excitation inequality; the result does not identify different subsequential limits.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-trial-contraction)): Let $N\ge4$ and $D\ge1$ be integers, and let $A_{-1},A_0,A_1\in M_D(\mathbb R)$. Define $\psi_N,T,O$ by Equations <a href="#eq:trial-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-vector">[eq:trial-vector]</a>–<a href="#eq:trial-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-insertion">[eq:trial-insertion]</a>. For the periodic spin-one Heisenberg Hamiltonian $H_N$, set $$V_N=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(T^N),\qquad U_N=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(T^{N-2}O).$$ Then $$\left\lVert \psi_N\right\rVert^2=V_N,\qquad \langle\psi_N,H_N\psi_N\rangle=NU_N.$$ In particular, if $V_N>0$ and $U_N<-aV_N$, then $E_0(N)<-Na$ and $Z_N(b)>1$ for every $b>0$.
  - Explanation: Exact finite matrix contractions give the norm and bond energy of a periodic matrix-product vector and a conditional variational certificate.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-trial-inputs)): For the untwisted periodic spin-one Heisenberg Hamiltonian and $a=700741/500000$, $$E_0(72)<-72a,\qquad E_0(120)<-120a.$$ Consequently $Z_{72}(b)>1$ and $Z_{120}(b)>1$ for every $b>0$.
  - Explanation: The explicit periodic trial family bounds the full ground energy and makes the shifted partition function exceed one.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-bootstrap",
        "exact_quote": "For every even $L\\ge4$, let $H_L$ be a non-scalar finite-dimensional self-adjoint Hamiltonian. Suppose that $a\\in\\mathbb R$ and, for each $b>0$, a real finite or countable list $(\\lambda_i(b))$ satisfy $$\\sum_i|\\lambda_i(b)|^4<\\infty,\\qquad\n Z_L(b):=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_L+aLI)}=\\sum_i|\\lambda_i(b)|^L\n \\quad(L\\ge4\\text{ even}).$$ Define $S,T$ by <a href=\"#eq:purities\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:purities\">[eq:purities]</a> and $G$ by <a href=\"#eq:bootstrap-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:bootstrap-coefficient\">[eq:bootstrap-coefficient]</a>. Let $n_0\\ge4$ be even, $b_0>0$, and let $u_0,C$ satisfy $$\\label{eq:bootstrap-hypotheses}\n 0<u_0<\\frac16,\\qquad G(u_0)\\le C,\\qquad\n r:=Cu_0<1,\\qquad C(1-3u_0)>3.$$ Suppose $$\\label{eq:bootstrap-seeds}\n S(n_0,b_0)\\ge1-u_0,\\qquad T(2n_0,b_0)\\ge1-u_0.$$ For $j\\ge0$, set $$\\label{eq:dyadic-parameters}\n n_j=2^j n_0,\\qquad b_j=2^j b_0,\\qquad\n u_j=C^{-1}r^{2^j}.$$ Then $$\\label{eq:dyadic-purities}\n S(n_j,b_j)\\ge1-u_j,\\qquad T(2n_j,b_j)\\ge1-u_j.$$ For every even $L\\ge n_0$, the physical ground state is unique, and the gap above it satisfies $$\\label{eq:bootstrap-gap}\n \\gamma_L>\\frac{\\log(1/r)}{b_0}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-268.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-cappedmass",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(t_i)$ be a finite or countable family with $0\\le t_i\\le h$ and $\\sum_i t_i\\le m$, where $h>0$ and $m\\ge0$. Without the individual cap, $\\sum_i t_i^r\\le m^r$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-polynomial-filter",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\epsilon\\ge0$, $v>0$, and let $(\\lambda_i)$ be a real eigenvalue list with moments $M_j=\\sum_i\\lambda_i^j$, and suppose $|M_j-c_j|\\le\\epsilon$ for the indices occurring in a polynomial $F(x)=\\sum_j f_jx^j$. Assume these moments converge absolutely and $F\\ge0$ on $\\mathbb R$. If $$B=\\sum_j f_jc_j+\\epsilon\\sum_j|f_j|\n \\quad\\hbox{and}\\quad F(x)>B\\ \\hbox{for }|x|\\ge v,$$ then every $|\\lambda_i|<v$.",
        "source_id": "PM-268.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-boundary-inputs",
        "exact_quote": "Let $X_b$ be the spatial transfer operator of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:transfer\">4</a>, with the shift $a=700741/500000$, and put $$\\begin{gathered}\n b_0=49/4,\\qquad b_1=49/2,\\\\\n \\ell=999/1000,\\qquad U=100139/100000,\\qquad V=872/1000,\\\\\n m_J=1.0657205,\\qquad m_N=.2783155.\n \\end{gathered}$$ At $b=b_0$, the full eigenvalue list, counted with multiplicity, is the $D_2$-invariant list $J$ together with three identical lists $N$. Every eigenvalue in $J$ has modulus less than $U$, and the sum of the twelfth powers of all eigenvalues in $J$ is at most $m_J$. Every eigenvalue in each $N$ list has modulus less than $V$, and that list’s twelfth powers sum to at most $m_N$. There is an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\\ell$. Moreover, $$1-S(72,b_1)\\le\\frac{151249}{2500000}.$$ For $n=72$ and $n=120$, one has $E_0(n)+na<0$; hence a physical ground Boltzmann factor in $Z_n(b)$ exceeds one for every $b>0$.",

      },
      "evidence": [

Every eigenvalue in $J$ has modulus less than $U$, and the sum of the twelfth powers of all eigenvalues in $J$ is at most $m_J$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-local-limit",
        "exact_quote": "For the self-adjoint Hamiltonians <a href=\"#eq:main-H\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:main-H\">[eq:main-H]</a>, the normalized ground states of the even periodic rings have a subsequence, as $L\\to\\infty$, converging on every local operator to a state $\\omega$. Every such limit satisfies $$\\label{eq:local-limit-gap}\n \\omega(A^*[H,A])\\ge\\frac{\\log20}{784}\n \\bigl(\\omega(A^*A)-|\\omega(A)|^2\\bigr)$$ for every local operator $A$.",
        "source_id": "PM-268.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-local-limit",
          "exact_quote": "For the self-adjoint Hamiltonians <a href=\"#eq:main-H\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:main-H\">[eq:main-H]</a>, the normalized ground states of the even periodic rings have a subsequence, as $L\\to\\infty$, converging on every local operator to a state $\\omega$. Every such limit satisfies $$\\label{eq:local-limit-gap}\n \\omega(A^*[H,A])\\ge\\frac{\\log20}{784}\n \\bigl(\\omega(A^*A)-|\\omega(A)|^2\\bigr)$$ for every local operator $A$.",
          "source_id": "PM-268.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "For the self-adjoint Hamiltonians <a href=\"#eq:main-H\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:main-H\">[eq:main-H]</a>, the normalized ground states of the even periodic rings have a subsequence, as $L\\to\\infty$, converging on every local operator to a state $\\omega$. Every such limit satisfies $$\\label{eq:local-limit-gap}\n \\omega(A^*[H,A])\\ge\\frac{\\log20}{784}\n \\bigl(\\omega(A^*A)-|\\omega(A)|^2\\bigr)$$ for every local operator $A$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-trial-contraction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $N\\ge4$ and $D\\ge1$ be integers, and let $A_{-1},A_0,A_1\\in M_D(\\mathbb R)$. For the periodic spin-one Heisenberg Hamiltonian $H_N$, set $$V_N=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Tr}}(T^N),\\qquad U_N=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Tr}}(T^{N-2}O).$$ Then $$\\left\\lVert \\psi_N\\right\\rVert^2=V_N,\\qquad\n \\langle\\psi_N,H_N\\psi_N\\rangle=NU_N.$$ In particular, if $V_N>0$ and $U_N<-aV_N$, then $E_0(N)<-Na$ and $Z_N(b)>1$ for every $b>0$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Periodic Hamiltonian** ([source](#eq:main-H)): For each even integer $L\ge4$, let $\mathcal H_L=(\mathbb C^3)^{\otimes L}$ and define $$\label{eq:main-H} H_L=\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}\sum_{\alpha=x,y,z} S_j^\alpha S_{j+1}^\alpha, \qquad S_L^\alpha=S_0^\alpha.$$
- **Spectral gap and even-periodic limit inferior** ([source](#eq:main-gap)): Write $E_0(L)$ for the least energy and $E_1(L)$ for the next *distinct* energy of $H_L$, and set $$\label{eq:main-gap} \gamma_L=E_1(L)-E_0(L), \qquad \Delta_1=\liminf_{\substack{L\to\infty\\L\text{ even}}}\gamma_L.$$
- **Twisted Hamiltonian** ([source](#eq:twisted-hamiltonian)): Define $$\label{eq:twisted-hamiltonian} H_n(g)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}h_{j,j+1}+h_{n-1,0}(g), \qquad h_{n-1,0}(g)=\sum_\alpha S_{n-1}^\alpha(gS^\alpha g^{-1})_0.$$
- **Shifted partition functions** ([source](#eq:shift)): Fix $$\label{eq:shift} a=\frac{700741}{500000},\qquad Z_n(b,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp[-b(H_n(g)+anI)],\qquad Z_n(b)=Z_n(b,I) \quad (b>0).$$
- **Spatial and physical purities** ([source](#eq:purities)): For even $n\ge4$, define the spatial and physical purities $$\label{eq:purities} S(n,b)=\frac{Z_{2n}(b)}{Z_n(b)^2},\qquad T(n,b)=\frac{Z_n(2b)}{Z_n(b)^2}.$$
- **Bootstrap coefficient** ([source](#eq:bootstrap-coefficient)): Put $$\label{eq:bootstrap-coefficient} G(u)=\frac12\left((1-u)^{-1}+(1-u)^{-2}+(1-u)^{-3}\right)^2.$$
- **Capped power-sum envelope** ([source](#stmt-lem-cappedmass)): For $r\ge1$, put $$\label{eq:capped-function} \mathcal C(m,h,r)=q h^r+(m-qh)^r,\qquad q=\lfloor m/h\rfloor.$$
- **Periodic matrix-product vector** ([source](#eq:trial-vector)): For $N\ge4$ and three real $D$-by-$D$ matrices $A_s$, indexed by $s=-1,0,1$, define a vector in the standard orthonormal spin-one weight basis by $$\label{eq:trial-vector} \psi_N(s_0,\ldots,s_{N-1}) =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(A_{s_0}\cdots A_{s_{N-1}}).$$
- **Local commutator** ([source](#pm-h-21)): Define the local commutator $$[H,A]=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z} [\mathbf S_j\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1},A].$$

          "exact_quote": "For the Hamiltonians <a href=\"#eq:main-H\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:main-H\">[eq:main-H]</a>, the ground state is unique for every even $L\\ge60$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\gamma_L&>\\frac4{105}\\log\\frac{80}{79}\n     &&\\text{for every even }L\\ge60,\\label{eq:gap60}\\\\\n \\gamma_L&>\\frac{\\log20}{784}\n     &&\\text{for every even }L\\ge2304.\\label{eq:gap2304}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$\\Delta_1\\ge\\frac{\\log20}{784}>0.$$ The spectral conclusions also hold for any simultaneously similar spin-one realization satisfying the same commutator and Casimir normalizations.",
          "source_id": "PM-268.1"
        }
      ],
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "id": "Q3",

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-representation): Ladder identities and simultaneous similarity. -> Standard spin-one realization.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-energy): Weighted absolute row sums on open triads. -> Uniform twisted energy enclosure.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-transfer): Ordered bond histories and Hilbert–Schmidt product trace identity. -> Spatial moment representation.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-sectors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sectors): Character projections and cyclic rotation symmetry. -> Sector moment lists and multiplicities.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-prop-bootstrap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-bootstrap): Coupled probability squaring and moment interpolation. -> Unique physical ground state and uniform gap.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-thermal-inputs) / [proof](#sec:thermal120): Conditioned Poisson polynomial with packed exact integer columns. -> First thermal trace enclosures.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-thermal-inputs) / [proof](#sec:thermal72): Integer column recurrence with rational trace endpoints. -> Second thermal trace enclosures.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-trial-inputs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-trial-inputs): Exact periodic matrix-product contractions and variational comparison. -> Negative shifted ground energies at the trial lengths.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-prop-seed60) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-seed60): Sector filters, capped masses, and dominant-weight bounds. -> First pair of purity seeds.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-prop-seed2304) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-seed2304): Polynomial filters and upward-rounded coupled updates. -> Stronger pair of purity seeds.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-boundary-inputs-2): Apply the common bootstrap to the finite initializations. -> Even-periodic gap theorem.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-cor-local-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-local-limit): Compactness and a diagonal subsequence followed by local limits. -> Local excitation inequality in each subsequential limit.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **rounded-update**: Upward rounding of the coupled update preserves the stated purity-defect bounds when its hypotheses and output range hold.
- **even-spatial**: Even spatial moments give nonnegative weights; spatial eigenvalues and physical energies remain different objects.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **odd**: Apply spatial probability concentration at odd lengths. -> Odd spatial powers need not be nonnegative.
- **boundary**: Treat the periodic gap as an open-chain boundary-field theorem. -> The boundary hypotheses require a separate argument.
- **certificate**: Treat agreement of scalar updates as a certificate for the full spectral theorem. -> The finite thermal and trial inputs are separate premises.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The paper gives an explicit finite rational procedure for the rounded coupled update. The implementation covers this scalar procedure; thermal traces, variational contractions and the analytic spectral conclusion remain separate obligations.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON pairs of rational strings and a bounded step count.
# PRECONDITIONS: Each defect lies in [0,1); steps is an integer from 1 through 6.
# OUTPUTS: Rounded rational defects, or REJECT when a precondition or output range fails.
# TIME: Linear in pairs times steps, apart from exact integer arithmetic cost.
# SPACE: Linear in output pairs, apart from exact integer arithmetic storage.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact python-flint fmpq and integer ceiling on a 10^7 grid.
# FAILURE: REJECT for invalid ranges; malformed JSON or rational syntax raises an error.
import sys, json
from flint import fmpq
SCALE = 10**7
def round_up(v):
    scaled = SCALE*v
    integer = (int(scaled.numerator)+int(scaled.denominator)-1)//int(scaled.denominator)
    return fmpq(integer, SCALE)
def update(p, q):
    defect = 1-(1-p)**2*(1-q)
    p_new = round_up(defect**2/(2*(1-defect)**2))
    if not 0 <= p_new < 1:
        return None
    defect = 1-(1-q)**2*(1-p_new)
    q_new = round_up(defect**2/(2*(1-defect)**2))
    if not 0 <= q_new < 1:
        return None
    return p_new, q_new
def solve(data):
    count = data['steps']
    result = []
    for pair in data['pairs']:
        p, q = map(fmpq, pair)
        if type(count) is not int or not 1 <= count <= 6 or not (0 <= p < 1 and 0 <= q < 1):
            result.append('REJECT'); continue
        for unused in range(count):
            next_pair = update(p, q)
            if next_pair is None:
                break
            p, q = next_pair
        result.append('REJECT' if next_pair is None else [str(p), str(q)])
    return result
print(json.dumps(solve(json.load(sys.stdin))))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The finite thermal calculations specify integer recurrences and rational trace enclosures that can be implemented as a certificate-producing numerical program. |
| games_simulation | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The explicit weight-basis Hamiltonians and thermal trace calculations support certified equilibrium calculations for the stated quantum spin chain. |
| tools | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The finite polynomial evaluation and explicit error accounting can serve as a validated thermal trace calculation tool for the supplied Hamiltonians. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The paper gives constructive polynomial filtering, capped-mass estimates and rounded coupled purity updates, alongside exact finite thermal and variational calculations. |
| data_structures | True | strong | implementable | inferred | Signed column batches are represented by packed arbitrary-precision integers, with bias and masking that preserve the intended coordinatewise floor. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The computation specifies integer arithmetic and batching, but establishes no accelerator architecture, device kernel or hardware performance contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The spectral and arithmetic certificates concern quantum spin Hamiltonians; no cryptographic primitive or security property is established. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The trial matrices are explicitly fixed and contracted; the paper supplies no training procedure, learned predictor or learning guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The variational calculation evaluates a fixed explicit trial family to bound a ground energy; it supplies no general optimisation procedure or operations research guarantee. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof and finite arithmetic procedures give no distributed protocol, communication model or fault-tolerance result. |
| other (Mathematical physics and quantum statistical mechanics) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | The central application is a uniform positive spectral gap for the pure antiferromagnetic spin-one Heisenberg chain on even periodic rings, with a local-excitation consequence for subsequential limits. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-1** (hypothesis): The explicit uniform gap and ground-state uniqueness conclusions concern even periodic rings of the pure bilinear spin-one model. Blocked use: ['Applying the periodic theorem directly to odd rings or open chains.']
- **limit-2** (hypothesis): The Hamiltonian has the stated spin-one normalization and coupling constant one. Changing the model requires a separate argument. Blocked use: ['Transferring the displayed constants to another spin, coupling normalization, or interaction without establishing the required correspondence.']
- **limit-3** (edge_case): At the remaining small even lengths, positivity of the gap above the full ground sector supplies neither an explicit numerical lower bound nor ground-state simplicity. Blocked use: ['Assigning the stated large-ring numerical bounds or uniqueness to every small even ring.']
- **limit-4** (formal_scope): The local excitation bound holds in each subsequential limit of periodic ground states; uniqueness among all infinite-volume ground states is not established. Blocked use: ['Claiming convergence to the same state for every subsequence.', 'Claiming uniqueness among all infinite-volume ground states.']
- **limit-5** (counterexample_scope): The even-periodic estimates do not by themselves establish the gap hypothesis for odd open chains with endpoint fields needed for the cited topological conclusion. Blocked use: ['Deducing the endpoint-field gap assumption or its topological conclusion solely from the periodic spectral gap.']
- **limit-6** (counterexample_scope): The cited stability result near the interaction with a biquadratic term does not establish a gapped path to the pure bilinear point. Blocked use: ['Treating local stability near that interaction as a proved gapped interpolation to the pure bilinear model.']
- **limit-7** (hypothesis): Even spatial powers provide the nonnegative probability distribution used by the purity argument. Odd moments retain signs and are used separately in polynomial estimates. Blocked use: ['Using an odd spatial moment as the nonnegative probability normalization required for the purity bootstrap.']
- **limit-8** (formal_scope): Spatial transfer eigenvalues and physical Hamiltonian energies are different objects. Sector information for the transfer operator does not restrict the physical spectrum. Blocked use: ['Identifying transfer eigenvalues with physical energies.', 'Discarding physical sectors when asserting a full-spectrum gap.']
- **limit-9** (hypothesis): Reusing the abstract bootstrap requires a non-scalar finite-dimensional self-adjoint Hamiltonian family and the stipulated spatial moment representation and purity seeds. Blocked use: ['Applying the criterion to a Hamiltonian family without proving its moment representation and seed hypotheses.']
- **limit-10** (approximation): The finite inputs require integer arithmetic, rational enclosures and explicit truncation errors. Floating-point eigenvalue estimates cannot replace those premises. Blocked use: ['Treating a floating-point spectral estimate as a certified thermal input.']
- **limit-11** (hypothesis): The spatial transfer construction does not require a positive transfer operator, and its invariant sector need not be one-dimensional. Blocked use: ['Assuming positivity of the transfer operator.', 'Assuming the invariant spatial sector is one-dimensional.']
- **limit-12** (approximation): Representative-column symmetries justify exact norm multiplicities; the rounded columns need not preserve those symmetries. Blocked use: ['Assuming rounding preserves the signed symmetry relations without the column error comparison.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove a uniform positive spectral gap for the pure antiferromagnetic spin-one Heisenberg chain on even periodic rings, establishing the positive even-periodic formulation of the spin-one Haldane conjecture.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Haldane predicted that antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chains have different low-energy behavior for integer and half-odd-integer spins. The prediction was first formulated in 1981 and published in 1983 (Haldane 1981, 1983b, 1983a) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Haldane1981](#ref-Haldane1981), [Haldane1983PRL](#ref-Haldane1983PRL), [Haldane1983PLA](#ref-Haldane1983PLA)]. For spin one, the prediction is a positive gap above the ground energy. We prove this prediction for the pure bilinear model on even periodic rings, with explicit bounds uniform in the length.

<a id="eq:main-H"></a>
<a id="eq:main-gap"></a>
Let $S^x,S^y,S^z\in M_3(\mathbb C)$ be the self-adjoint irreducible spin-one matrices, normalized by $$[S^\alpha,S^\beta]
   =i\sum_\eta\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\eta}S^\eta,
 \qquad \sum_\alpha(S^\alpha)^2=2I_3.$$ For each even integer $L\ge4$, let $\mathcal H_L=(\mathbb C^3)^{\otimes L}$ and define $$\label{eq:main-H}
 H_L=\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}\sum_{\alpha=x,y,z}
       S_j^\alpha S_{j+1}^\alpha,
 \qquad S_L^\alpha=S_0^\alpha.$$ Here $S_j^\alpha$ acts on site $j$ and is the identity on the other factors. The coupling constant is one. Write $E_0(L)$ for the least energy and $E_1(L)$ for the next *distinct* energy of $H_L$, and set $$\label{eq:main-gap}
 \gamma_L=E_1(L)-E_0(L),
 \qquad
 \Delta_1=\liminf_{\substack{L\to\infty\\L\text{ even}}}\gamma_L.$$ These definitions concern the full spectrum, including the multiplicity of the ground energy. The next distinct energy exists: the all-zero and all-$+1$ product vectors in the $S^z$ basis have energy expectations $0$ and $L$, respectively, so $H_L$ is not scalar.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For the Hamiltonians <a href="#eq:main-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-H">[eq:main-H]</a>, the ground state is unique for every even $L\ge60$, and <a id="eq:gap60"></a><a id="eq:gap2304"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \gamma_L&>\frac4{105}\log\frac{80}{79}
     &&\text{for every even }L\ge60,\label{eq:gap60}\\
 \gamma_L&>\frac{\log20}{784}
     &&\text{for every even }L\ge2304.\label{eq:gap2304}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $$\Delta_1\ge\frac{\log20}{784}>0.$$ The spectral conclusions also hold for any simultaneously similar spin-one realization satisfying the same commutator and Casimir normalizations.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the spin-one Haldane gap conjecture positively in the even-periodic finite-volume formulation $\Delta_1>0$. It also implies $\inf_{L\ge4,\,L\text{ even}}\gamma_L>0$: each of the finitely many remaining finite-dimensional Hamiltonians has a positive gap above its full ground sector. This observation supplies neither a numerical value nor a simplicity assertion at those small lengths. Corollary <a href="#cor:local-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:local-limit">15</a> also gives the corresponding lower bound for local excitations in every subsequential thermodynamic limit of the periodic ground states of the self-adjoint model.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context

The half-odd-integer side has a rigorous antecedent in the low-energy excitation bound of Lieb, Schultz and Mattis for the cyclic spin-one-half chain (Lieb, Schultz, and Mattis 1961, Appendix B, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LiebSchultzMattis1961](#ref-LiebSchultzMattis1961)]. Affleck and Lieb extended this obstruction to the half-odd-integer antiferromagnetic chains they studied (Affleck and Lieb 1986) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[AffleckLieb1986](#ref-AffleckLieb1986)]. The possible alternative of degenerate ground states is part of that obstruction. Integer spin permits a unique gapped state, but this distinction alone does not prove a gap for a particular interaction.

The Affleck–Kennedy–Lieb–Tasaki construction established a rigorous gapped spin-one chain with a biquadratic interaction (Affleck et al. 1987, 1988) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[AKLT1987](#ref-AKLT1987), [AKLT1988](#ref-AKLT1988)]. Its bond interaction is $h+\tfrac13h^2$, up to a scalar and normalization, where $h=\mathbf S_j\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1}$. The Hamiltonian in <a href="#eq:main-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-H">[eq:main-H]</a> has bond interaction $h$ itself. Yarotsky proved that a unique ground state and a uniform periodic gap persist under sufficiently small translation-invariant finite-range perturbations of the AKLT interaction (Yarotsky 2006, arXiv v1, Theorems 1 and 3) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Yarotsky2006](#ref-Yarotsky2006)]. This controls a neighborhood of that interaction; it does not establish a gapped path to the pure bilinear point.

For the pure spin-one chain, White and Huse’s density-matrix renormalization-group study estimated a bulk gap of $0.41050(2)$ and exhibited effective spin-one-half degrees of freedom at open ends (White and Huse 1993) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[WhiteHuse1993](#ref-WhiteHuse1993)]. Neutron scattering provided experimental evidence in the nearly one-dimensional spin-one antiferromagnet $\mathrm{CsNiCl}_3$ (Buyers et al. 1986) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Buyers1986](#ref-Buyers1986)]. These results explain both the strong physical evidence for the prediction and the significance of boundary conditions. The uniform lower bounds below instead follow from the stated finite certificates and an analytic argument valid at every larger even length.

Batista and Ortiz proposed a spin–fermion proof of the Haldane gap (Batista and Ortiz 2001) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BatistaOrtiz2001](#ref-BatistaOrtiz2001)]. Their gap argument following Equation (8) of the preprint version uses a first-order perturbative identification with an XYZ chain; that step does not supply a uniform remainder estimate or a continuation to the original pairing coefficient. More recently, Tasaki established a nontrivial infinite-chain topological index for the spin-one Heisenberg model under a gap assumption for odd open chains with endpoint fields (Tasaki 2025, arXiv v4, Assumption 2 and Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)]. The boundary hypotheses of that theorem require a separate argument from the even-periodic estimates proved here.

Finite-volume methods for uniform spectral gaps have a substantial history, including the criteria of Knabe and Nachtergaele for frustration-free chains, whose ground states minimize every local interaction (Knabe 1988; Nachtergaele 1996) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Knabe1988](#ref-Knabe1988), [Nachtergaele1996](#ref-Nachtergaele1996)]. Gosset and Mozgunov obtained sharper finite-size thresholds within that frustration-free setting (Gosset and Mozgunov 2016) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GossetMozgunov2016](#ref-GossetMozgunov2016)]. Recently, Banerjee, Guo and Chowdhury developed semidefinite bounds for string correlators using an enclosing ground-energy window, including finite periodic spin-one Heisenberg chains (Banerjee, Guo, and Chowdhury 2026, Equations (2), (13) and Figure 4) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BanerjeeGuoChowdhury2026](#ref-BanerjeeGuoChowdhury2026)]. Those observable bounds concern a different quantity from the uniform spectral gap studied here. Our finite inputs are thermal traces of small twisted chains and variational energy estimates from periodic matrix-product vectors, in the tradition of finitely correlated states (Fannes, Nachtergaele, and Werner 1992) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[FNW1992](#ref-FNW1992)]. The latter use the standard trace-of-matrix-products representation (Pérez-García et al. 2007, sec. 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[PerezGarcia2007](#ref-PerezGarcia2007)]; we give the particular integer matrices and all contractions explicitly.

The finite thermal inputs are rigorous enclosures of matrix-exponential traces. Validated matrix exponentiation has a long history, including self-validating Padé methods (Bochev and Markov 1989) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BochevMarkov1989](#ref-BochevMarkov1989)] and interval polynomial evaluation (Goldsztejn and Neumaier 2014) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GoldsztejnNeumaier2014](#ref-GoldsztejnNeumaier2014)]. Our particular implementation uses a conditioned Poisson polynomial in a contraction. The factor-two conditioning estimate follows the coefficient argument of Fox and Glynn (Fox and Glynn 1988, Proposition 1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[FoxGlynn1988](#ref-FoxGlynn1988)]. Explicit bounds for the integer evaluation and the final trace enclosure are supplied in the appendices.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### How the proof works

Quantum transfer-matrix methods relate partition functions to spatial moments (Suzuki 1985) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Suzuki1985](#ref-Suzuki1985)]. Our construction uses an ordered bond expansion of the type developed by Aizenman and Nachtergaele (Aizenman and Nachtergaele 1994, arXiv v1, Section 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[AizenmanNachtergaele1994](#ref-AizenmanNachtergaele1994)], followed by a direct operator construction on bond histories.

A shifted partition function is a sum of positive physical Boltzmann weights. We construct a self-adjoint spatial transfer operator whose $L$th moment is the same partition function. For even $L$, the spatial moment is also a sum of nonnegative weights. This gives two probability distributions associated with one partition function. The *purity* of a distribution $(p_i)$ is $\sum_i p_i^2$; purity close to one means that a single weight carries almost all of its mass.

Squaring and renormalizing a distribution reduces its purity defect quadratically once the defect is small. Squaring physical weights doubles inverse temperature, while squaring spatial weights doubles length. Exact cancellation identities couple these two improvements. Starting from two finite purity bounds, both defects then decay doubly exponentially as length and inverse temperature double together. Spatial moment inequalities cover every intermediate even length. Finally, purity of the full physical Gibbs distribution bounds each excited-to-ground probability ratio and hence the physical energy gap. Proposition <a href="#prop:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bootstrap">8</a> states this coupled argument as a general gap criterion. Its assumptions isolate a reusable criterion for other Hamiltonian families with the same two partition-function representations; positivity of the spatial transfer is not required.

There are two finite initializations. One starts the iteration at length $60$ and inverse temperature $105/4$. The other reaches length $2304$ and inverse temperature $784$ with a smaller purity defect, giving the stronger eventual bound. They share the transfer construction and the bootstrap, while using different thermal moments and two lengths of one matrix-product trial family. The finite computations involve integer arithmetic, rational interval bounds and explicit truncation errors; no floating-point eigenvalue estimate is a premise.

Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">2</a> fixes the model and proves its elementary energy bounds. Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">3</a> constructs the spatial transfer and its rotation sectors. Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">4</a> proves the common purity theorem, and Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">5</a> establishes the two finite initializations from explicitly stated thermal and trial inputs. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">6</a> combines these results to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Appendices <a href="#sec:thermal120" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:thermal120">7</a>–<a href="#sec:trials" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trials">9</a> prove the essential thermal and trial inputs, with exact data and reproducible finite procedures.

<a id="sec:model"></a>
### The Hamiltonian and its auxiliary twists

The argument uses the same partition function in two different ways: as a Gibbs trace in the physical Hilbert space and as a moment of a spatial operator. We first choose coordinates that make both descriptions explicit. We also establish an elementary energy bound used to control the finite exponential approximations.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Spin coordinates

We use the self-adjoint spin-one matrices. This convention does not restrict the spectrum in the formulation where self-adjointness is not assumed.

<a id="stmt-lem-representation"></a>
<div id="lem:representation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Choice of representation). *Let $S^x,S^y,S^z\in M_3(\mathbb C)$ satisfy $$[S^\alpha,S^\beta]
   =i\sum_\gamma\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}S^\gamma,
 \qquad \sum_\alpha(S^\alpha)^2=2I.$$ They are simultaneously similar to the standard self-adjoint spin-one matrices. If the original matrices are self-adjoint, the similarity can be chosen unitary. The corresponding tensor-power similarity preserves the complete spectrum of every periodic Hamiltonian $H_n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-representation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-representation</summary>

<a id="eq:ladder-identities"></a>
*Proof.* Put $S^\pm=S^x\pm iS^y$. The assumptions give $$\label{eq:ladder-identities}
 [S^z,S^\pm]=\pm S^\pm,\qquad
 S^-S^+=2I-(S^z)^2-S^z,\qquad
 S^+S^-=2I-(S^z)^2+S^z.$$ Choose an $S^z$ eigenvector $v$ whose eigenvalue $m$ has maximal real part. Then $S^+v=0$, since otherwise it has eigenvalue $m+1$. Thus $m=1$ or $m=-2$. The latter case would permit indefinite nonzero lowering: a last nonzero lowered vector would have weight $-2-k$, whereas $S^+S^-w=0$ forces its weight to be $-1$ or $2$. This contradicts finite dimension. Hence $m=1$.

The vectors $$v,\qquad \frac{S^-v}{\sqrt2},\qquad \frac{(S^-)^2v}{2}$$ are nonzero by <a href="#eq:ladder-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ladder-identities">[eq:ladder-identities]</a> and have distinct weights $1,0,-1$. They form a basis. There is no further lowered vector, because the basis has no weight $-2$. The identities give raising and lowering coefficients $\sqrt2$, so this is the standard spin-one representation. If the original matrices are self-adjoint and $\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1$, the three vectors are orthonormal. Finally, conjugating each tensor factor conjugates every term of $H_n$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:cartesian-bond"></a>
In the weight basis $|s\rangle$, $s\in\{-1,0,1\}$, the diagonal generator is $S^z|s\rangle=s|s\rangle$ and the nonzero ladder entries are $\sqrt2$. We will also use the Cartesian basis, indexed by the three axes, in which $$(S^\alpha)_{ij}=-i\varepsilon_{\alpha i j},\qquad
 G_\alpha=iS^\alpha.$$ These matrices satisfy the same relations. Each $G_\alpha$ is real and has operator norm one. For the two-site interaction $h=\sum_\alpha S^\alpha\otimes S^\alpha$, contraction of the epsilon tensors gives $$\label{eq:cartesian-bond}
 -h=\sum_\alpha G_\alpha\otimes G_\alpha,
 \qquad h=F-|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|,
 \qquad \Omega=\sum_{i=1}^3|ii\rangle,$$ where $F(u\otimes v)=v\otimes u$. In particular, $h$ has eigenvalues $-2,-1,1$, and $h\le I$.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Twists and partition functions

<a id="eq:twisted-hamiltonian"></a>
<a id="eq:shift"></a>
Number the sites $0,\ldots,n-1$, with $n\ge4$. A proper Cartesian rotation $g\in SO(3)$ is unitary on $\mathbb C^3$. Define $$\label{eq:twisted-hamiltonian}
 H_n(g)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}h_{j,j+1}+h_{n-1,0}(g),
 \qquad
 h_{n-1,0}(g)=\sum_\alpha
 S_{n-1}^\alpha(gS^\alpha g^{-1})_0.$$ Thus only the last bond is conjugated, on its site-$0$ factor. The Hamiltonian is self-adjoint, and $H_n(I)=H_n$. Fix $$\label{eq:shift}
 a=\frac{700741}{500000},\qquad
 Z_n(b,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp[-b(H_n(g)+anI)],\qquad Z_n(b)=Z_n(b,I)
 \quad (b>0).$$ All these partition functions are strictly positive. The scalar shift will cancel from the purity ratios and from the final spectral gap. With this normalization, the trial bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:trial-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trial-inputs">17</a> give $E_0(n)+an<0$ at $n=72,120$, and hence $Z_n(b)>1$ for every $b>0$. These lower bounds are paired with finite thermal upper bounds in Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">5</a>.

We need the diagonal rotations $$P_x=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,-1),\quad
 P_y=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,-1),\quad
 P_z=\operatorname{diag}(-1,-1,1),\qquad
 D_2=\{I,P_x,P_y,P_z\},$$ and the cyclic rotation $C:e_x\mapsto e_y\mapsto e_z\mapsto e_x$. Write $P=P_x$. The three nonidentity elements of $D_2$ have equal partition functions, by a global cyclic rotation of all sites. The rotation $C$ has angle $2\pi/3$ about the diagonal axis. After a global rotation and a change to the weight basis, $P$ and $C$ are respectively represented by $\exp(-i\theta S^z)$ with $\theta=\pi$ and $\theta=2\pi/3$.

<a id="eq:seam-phase"></a>
For later comparison with finite matrices, a word $x=(x_0,\ldots,x_{n-1})$ in the weight basis has diagonal entry $\sum_j x_{j-1}x_j$, with indices modulo $n$. Each permitted step changing two neighboring letters by opposite units has coefficient one. For a seam step from column $y$ to row $x$, this coefficient is $$\label{eq:seam-phase}
 \exp\bigl(i\theta(y_0-x_0)\bigr).$$ Indeed $gS^\pm g^{-1}=e^{\mp i\theta}S^\pm$. These formulas identify the finite matrices used below with the physical Hamiltonians in <a href="#eq:twisted-hamiltonian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:twisted-hamiltonian">[eq:twisted-hamiltonian]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A uniform energy bound

<a id="stmt-lem-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:energy-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Energy bounds). *For every $n\ge4$ and every $g\in SO(3)$, $$\label{eq:energy-bounds}
 -\frac{3n}{2}I\le H_n(g)\le nI.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The upper bound follows from $h\le I$ and unitary conjugation of the seam. For the lower bound, consider $T=-h_{12}-h_{23}$ on three open sites in Cartesian coordinates. By <a href="#eq:cartesian-bond" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartesian-bond">[eq:cartesian-bond]</a>, an unequal pair swaps with coefficient $-1$, and an equal pair changes to either of the other equal pairs with coefficient $1$. There are no diagonal terms.

Assign a positive weight $q$ to each three-letter word. The following table lists every equality pattern; $a,b,c$ denote distinct letters. The last column sums the weights of the neighbors of a row word, with the absolute matrix coefficients.

<div class="center">

|   word type    | $q$ | absolute weighted row sum |
|:--------------:|:---:|:-------------------------:|
|     $aaa$      | $4$ |       $3+3+3+3=12$        |
| $aab$ or $abb$ | $3$ |         $4+3+2=9$         |
|     $aba$      | $2$ |          $3+3=6$          |
|     $abc$      | $2$ |          $2+2=4$          |

</div>

Every row sum is at most $3q$. If $D$ is the diagonal matrix of these weights, $D^{-1}TD$ has absolute row sums at most three. Its spectral radius is therefore at most three. Since $T$ is self-adjoint and similar to this matrix, $h_{12}+h_{23}\ge-3I$.

An open triad containing the seam is unitarily equivalent to an untwisted triad: delete the seam bond and rotate all sites in one of the two resulting components. The internal interaction of that component is rotation invariant. Thus the same lower bound holds for every consecutive pair of bonds in $H_n(g)$. Summing the $n$ triad inequalities counts each bond twice and proves <a href="#eq:energy-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-bounds">[eq:energy-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### Partition functions as spatial moments

We now construct a self-adjoint spatial operator whose moments are the partition functions <a href="#eq:shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shift">[eq:shift]</a>. This identity lets even spatial powers play the same role as positive Gibbs weights. The operator itself need not be positive.

<a id="eq:rotation-covariance"></a>
Let $\mathcal G\subset SO(3)$ be the group of proper signed permutation matrices. It contains $D_2$ and $C$. For $g\in\mathcal G$, write $$g e_\alpha=\epsilon_g(\alpha)e_{\sigma_g(\alpha)},
 \qquad \epsilon_g(\alpha)\in\{-1,1\}.$$ Rotation covariance of the Cartesian matrices gives $$\label{eq:rotation-covariance}
 gG_\alpha g^{-1}
   =\epsilon_g(\alpha)G_{\sigma_g(\alpha)}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:transfer" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:spatial-trace"></a>
<a id="eq:spatial-moments"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Spatial transfer). *For each $b>0$ there are a Hilbert space $\mathcal K_b$, a compact self-adjoint operator $X_b$, and a unitary representation $g\mapsto U_g$ of $\mathcal G$ commuting with $X_b$, such that $$\label{eq:spatial-trace}
 Z_n(b,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X_b^nU_g)
 \qquad(n\ge4,\ g\in\mathcal G).$$ The operator $X_b$ and the representation commute with complex conjugation. Moreover $X_b$ is Hilbert–Schmidt, so $X_b^k$ is trace class for every integer $k\ge2$. If $\lambda_i(b)$ are its nonzero eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, then $$\label{eq:spatial-moments}
 \sum_i|\lambda_i(b)|^k<\infty\quad(k\ge2),\qquad
 Z_n(b)=\sum_i\lambda_i(b)^n\quad(n\ge4).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* *Histories and the kernel.* A bond history is a finite ordered list $$x=((t_1,\alpha_1),\ldots,(t_k,\alpha_k)),
 \qquad 0<t_1<\cdots<t_k<b,\quad
 \alpha_j\in\{x,y,z\},$$ including the empty list when $k=0$. Let $\Omega_b$ be the disjoint union of these labeled simplexes. Give each simplex Lebesgue measure and the empty history mass one. The resulting measure $\mu$ is finite: <a id="eq:history-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:history-mass}
 \mu(\Omega_b)=\sum_{k=0}^\infty\frac{3^kb^k}{k!}=e^{3b}.$$ Set $\mathcal K_b=L^2(\Omega_b,\mu;\mathbb C)$.

For two histories $x,y$, merge their labeled times in increasing order and define $k_b(x,y)$ as the trace of the resulting product of the matrices $G_\alpha$, with increasing time from left to right. A nonempty time collision is a null event; set the kernel to zero there. The product for two empty histories is the identity, so its trace is three.

The kernel is real and has absolute value at most three, since $\left\lVert G_\alpha\right\rVert=1$. Exchanging $x$ and $y$ leaves their merged chronological list unchanged. Hence $k_b(x,y)=k_b(y,x)$; no reversal of the matrix product occurs. Its integral operator $K_b$ is therefore self-adjoint and Hilbert–Schmidt, with $$\left\lVert K_b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le9\mu(\Omega_b)^2=9e^{6b}.$$ Define $X_b=e^{-ab}K_b$. Compact self-adjoint spectral theory gives real, square-summable eigenvalues. Since they are bounded, all their absolute moments of integer order at least two are summable. This proves the operator assertions about $X_b$.

<a id="eq:twisted-kernel"></a>
*Rotations.* The permutation $\sigma_g$ acts on the labels of a history, preserving its times and the measure. Put $$s_g(x)=\prod_{\alpha\text{ in }x}\epsilon_g(\alpha),\qquad
 (U_g f)(x)=s_g(\sigma_g^{-1}x)f(\sigma_g^{-1}x).$$ The rule $s_{gh}(x)=s_g(\sigma_hx)s_h(x)$ proves that these real unitaries form a representation. Simultaneously rotating every matrix inside a site trace, using <a href="#eq:rotation-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-covariance">[eq:rotation-covariance]</a>, gives $$k_b(\sigma_gx,\sigma_gy)=s_g(x)s_g(y)k_b(x,y).$$ It follows that $U_g$ commutes with $K_b$ and $X_b$. In particular the kernel of $K_bU_g$ is $$\label{eq:twisted-kernel}
 (K_bU_g)(x,y)=k_b(x,\sigma_gy)s_g(y).$$

<a id="eq:cycle-integral"></a>
*The cycle trace.* For $n\ge2$, both $K_b^{n-1}$ and $K_bU_g$ are Hilbert–Schmidt. The kernel of $K_b^{n-1}$ is the iterated kernel obtained by integration; it is bounded because $k_b$ is bounded and $\mu$ is finite. The Hilbert–Schmidt product trace identity $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(AB)=\int a(x,y)b(y,x)\,d\mu(x)\,d\mu(y)$$ and <a href="#eq:twisted-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:twisted-kernel">[eq:twisted-kernel]</a> consequently give $$\label{eq:cycle-integral}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(K_b^nU_g)=
 \int_{\Omega_b^n}
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-2}k_b(x_j,x_{j+1})\right)
 k_b(x_{n-1},\sigma_gx_0)s_g(x_0)\,d\mu^n.$$ The product trace identity follows from the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product formula and Cauchy–Schwarz. All the iterated kernel integrals here are absolutely integrable. Thus <a href="#eq:cycle-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-integral">[eq:cycle-integral]</a> does not require a diagonal-kernel formula for $K_b$ itself.

*Identification with the physical partition function.* The ordered bond expansion is a standard representation of spin partition functions (Aizenman and Nachtergaele 1994, arXiv v1, Section 2.2, Equations (2.10)–(2.12)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[AizenmanNachtergaele1994](#ref-AizenmanNachtergaele1994)]. We verify it here with the required seam and normalization. Expand $\exp[-bH_n(g)]$ in its ordered-time series. By <a href="#eq:cartesian-bond" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cartesian-bond">[eq:cartesian-bond]</a>, each event chooses a bond and one of three labels, and inserts the corresponding two $G$ factors. At the seam the site-$0$ factor is rotated as in <a href="#eq:rotation-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-covariance">[eq:rotation-covariance]</a>. There are $(3n)^k$ choices at total degree $k$; each tensor operator has norm at most one. The sum of the absolute traced contributions is bounded by <a id="eq:history-absolute"></a>$$\label{eq:history-absolute}
 3^n\sum_{k=0}^\infty\frac{(3nb)^k}{k!}=3^ne^{3nb}.$$ We may therefore regroup all terms and integrals by bond. The interleavings of the bond histories partition the global time simplex up to null collisions. At each site, the tensor trace is the kernel on the two incident histories, in their original chronological order.

For an explicit check of the seam, call its history $e_0$ and enumerate the remaining bond histories $e_1,\ldots,e_{n-1}$ around the ring. The physical integrand is $$s_g(e_0)k_b(\sigma_ge_0,e_1)
 k_b(e_1,e_2)\cdots k_b(e_{n-1},e_0).$$ In <a href="#eq:cycle-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-integral">[eq:cycle-integral]</a>, set $x_0=e_0,x_1=e_{n-1},\ldots,x_{n-1}=e_1$ and use kernel symmetry. This gives exactly the displayed integrand, with $g$ on site $0$. The spatial enumeration has been reversed; the chronological products have not. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-bH_n(g)}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(K_b^nU_g)$. Multiplication by $e^{-abn}$ proves <a href="#eq:spatial-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-trace">[eq:spatial-trace]</a>. The ordinary trace formula for a compact self-adjoint operator now gives <a href="#eq:spatial-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-moments">[eq:spatial-moments]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:spatial-probabilities"></a>
For an even $n\ge4$, Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a> supplies the probabilities $$\label{eq:spatial-probabilities}
 p_i=\frac{|\lambda_i(b)|^n}{Z_n(b)},\qquad
 \sum_i p_i=1,\qquad
 \sum_i p_i^2=\frac{Z_{2n}(b)}{Z_n(b)^2}.$$ Odd moments will also be useful in polynomial estimates, but evenness is essential in <a href="#eq:spatial-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-probabilities">[eq:spatial-probabilities]</a>. These spatial eigenvalues are distinct objects from the physical energies of $H_n$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Rotation sectors

The small twisted partition functions separate the spatial eigenvalues into a few lists. Their multiplicities will help isolate a dominant weight; no restriction on the physical spectrum is imposed.

<a id="stmt-lem-sectors"></a>
<div id="lem:sectors" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sector-total"></a>
<a id="eq:sector-pi"></a>
<a id="eq:sector-cyclic"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Sector moments). *Fix $b>0$ and let $X_b,U_g$ be as in Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>. There are real eigenvalue lists, counted with multiplicity, whose integer moments $I_k,O_k,N_k$ converge absolutely for $k\ge2$, with $$\begin{aligned}
 Z_n(b)&=I_n+2O_n+3N_n,\label{eq:sector-total}\\
 Z_n(b,P)&=I_n+2O_n-N_n,\label{eq:sector-pi}\\
 Z_n(b,C)&=I_n-O_n\qquad(n\ge4).\label{eq:sector-cyclic}
\end{aligned}$$ The $I$ list is the restriction to the joint invariant subspace of $D_2$ and $C$. The $O$ list occurs twice and the $N$ list three times in the full eigenvalue list of $X_b$. All three moments are nonnegative at even orders. If $J_n$ denotes the moment on the $D_2$-invariant subspace, then $$J_n=I_n+2O_n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sectors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sectors</summary>

*Proof.* The four characters $\chi$ of $D_2$ define orthogonal projections $$\Pi_\chi=\frac14\sum_{g\in D_2}\chi(g)U_g.$$ They commute with $X_b$ and sum to the identity. Conjugation by $U_C$ permutes the three nontrivial character spaces cyclically. The restrictions of $X_b$ to these spaces are therefore unitarily equivalent; denote their common moment by $N_k$.

In the trivial-character space, split further according to the eigenvalues $1,\omega,\overline\omega$ of $U_C$, where $\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}$. These are reducing subspaces for $X_b$. Complex conjugation exchanges the last two spaces and preserves every real eigenvalue of $X_b$, with multiplicity. Their common moment is $O_k$; the moment on the first space is $I_k$. Absolute convergence follows from <a href="#eq:spatial-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-moments">[eq:spatial-moments]</a>.

This orthogonal decomposition proves <a href="#eq:sector-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-total">[eq:sector-total]</a>. On the three nontrivial character spaces, a fixed $U_P$ has signs $+1,-1,-1$, while it is the identity on the trivial-character space. This gives <a href="#eq:sector-pi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-pi">[eq:sector-pi]</a>. The operator $U_C$ permutes the nontrivial spaces, so they contribute zero to $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X_b^nU_C)$. On the remaining three spaces its coefficients are $1,\omega,\overline\omega$, and $\omega+\overline\omega=-1$. This proves <a href="#eq:sector-cyclic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-cyclic">[eq:sector-cyclic]</a>. The identity for $J_n$ follows from the decomposition of the trivial-character space; even powers give nonnegativity. ◻

</details>

The invariant $I$ space need not be one-dimensional. The point of Lemma <a href="#lem:sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sectors">5</a> is that every weight outside it occurs at least twice in the full list. Later moment bounds will identify a unique largest weight without discarding any sector.

<a id="sec:bootstrap"></a>
### From two purities to a uniform gap

The spatial and physical descriptions of the same partition function give two ways to concentrate its weights. This section isolates the argument that propagates two initial concentration estimates to all larger even lengths. The initial estimates are established in Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">5</a>.

By Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>, each partition function is both a physical Gibbs trace and, at even lengths, a nonnegative spatial moment. The former counts every physical eigenvector, with multiplicity; the latter counts the absolute powers of every spatial eigenvalue. These two representations give the two probability distributions used below.

<a id="eq:purities"></a>
For even $n\ge4$, define the spatial and physical purities $$\label{eq:purities}
 S(n,b)=\frac{Z_{2n}(b)}{Z_n(b)^2},\qquad
 T(n,b)=\frac{Z_n(2b)}{Z_n(b)^2}.$$ The energy shift $aL$ cancels from both ratios. We first record the elementary inequality responsible for their improvement under squaring.

<a id="stmt-lem-purity"></a>
<div id="lem:purity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:two-squarings"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Squaring probabilities). *Let $(w_i)$ be a finite or countable family of nonnegative numbers with $\sum_iw_i=1$. Its purity $P=\sum_iw_i^2$ lies in $(0,1]$. The normalized squares $w_i^2/P$ have purity $P'$ satisfying <a id="eq:purity-square"></a>$$\label{eq:purity-square}
 1-P'\le f(1-P),\qquad
 f(u)=\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)^2}\quad(0\le u<1).$$ The function $f$ is increasing. Consequently $$\label{eq:two-squarings}
 \begin{split}
 0<S(n,b),T(n,b)&\le1,\\
 1-S(2n,b)&\le f(1-S(n,b)),\\
 1-T(n,2b)&\le f(1-T(n,b)).
 \end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-purity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-purity</summary>

*Proof.* At least one weight is positive, so $P>0$, and $w_i^2\le w_i$ gives $P\le1$. Nonnegativity permits rearrangement of all countable sums. Thus $$1-P'
 =\frac{2\sum_{i<j}w_i^2w_j^2}{P^2}
 \le\frac{2\bigl(\sum_{i<j}w_iw_j\bigr)^2}{P^2}
 =\frac{(1-P)^2}{2P^2}.$$ Also $f'(u)=u/(1-u)^3\ge0$.

<a id="eq:gibbs-weights"></a>
For $S(n,b)$, use the spatial probabilities $|\lambda_i(b)|^n/Z_n(b)$ from <a href="#eq:spatial-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-probabilities">[eq:spatial-probabilities]</a>. Squaring them changes $n$ to $2n$. For $T(n,b)$, list all physical eigenvalues $\varepsilon_k(n)$ with multiplicity and use $$\label{eq:gibbs-weights}
 w_k(n,b)=\frac{e^{-b(\varepsilon_k(n)+an)}}{Z_n(b)}.$$ Their purity is $T(n,b)$, and squaring them changes $b$ to $2b$. This proves <a href="#eq:two-squarings" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-squarings">[eq:two-squarings]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The coupled update

<a id="eq:space-time-cancel"></a>
<a id="eq:time-space-cancel"></a>
The two purities constrain one another because their definitions use the same partition functions. Direct cancellation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 S(n,2b)&=S(n,b)^2\frac{T(2n,b)}{T(n,b)^2},
   \label{eq:space-time-cancel}\\
 T(4n,b)&=T(2n,b)^2\frac{S(2n,2b)}{S(2n,b)^2}.
   \label{eq:time-space-cancel}
\end{aligned}$$ Both denominators are at most one. The following form allows the initial bounds to have different sizes and to be rounded upward.

<a id="stmt-lem-rounded-update"></a>
<div id="lem:rounded-update" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Coupled update). *Suppose $n\ge4$ is even, $b>0$, and $p,q\in[0,1)$ satisfy $$1-S(n,b)\le p,\qquad 1-T(2n,b)\le q.$$ If $p_+,q_+\in[0,1)$ obey <a id="eq:rounded-update"></a>$$\label{eq:rounded-update}
 \begin{split}
 p_+&\ge f\bigl(1-(1-p)^2(1-q)\bigr),\\
 q_+&\ge f\bigl(1-(1-q)^2(1-p_+)\bigr),
 \end{split}$$ then $$1-S(2n,2b)\le p_+,\qquad
 1-T(4n,2b)\le q_+.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rounded-update"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rounded-update</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#eq:space-time-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:space-time-cancel">[eq:space-time-cancel]</a> gives $S(n,2b)\ge(1-p)^2(1-q)$. Applying spatial squaring in Lemma <a href="#lem:purity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:purity">6</a> and monotonicity of $f$ proves the first bound. Next <a href="#eq:time-space-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:time-space-cancel">[eq:time-space-cancel]</a> gives $T(4n,b)\ge(1-q)^2(1-p_+)$. Temporal squaring proves the second. Every input of $f$ is in $[0,1)$ because the product subtracted from one is positive and at most one. ◻

</details>

In particular, one may round the first output upward before using it in the second update, then round the second output upward. The pair based at $(n,b)$ becomes a pair based at $(2n,2b)$: its physical member is at length $4n$. No decrease of the individual bounds is needed for this step.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Quadratic decay and intermediate lengths

<a id="eq:bootstrap-coefficient"></a>
Once both defects are small, one common bound suffices. Put $$\label{eq:bootstrap-coefficient}
 G(u)=\frac12\left((1-u)^{-1}+(1-u)^{-2}+(1-u)^{-3}\right)^2.$$ This function is increasing on $[0,1)$, and <a id="eq:bootstrap-factorization"></a>$$\label{eq:bootstrap-factorization}
 f\bigl(1-(1-u)^3\bigr)=G(u)u^2.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-bootstrap"></a>
<div id="prop:bootstrap" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:bootstrap-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:bootstrap-seeds"></a>
<a id="eq:dyadic-purities"></a>
<a id="eq:bootstrap-gap"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Uniform gap from two initial purities). *For every even $L\ge4$, let $H_L$ be a non-scalar finite-dimensional self-adjoint Hamiltonian. Suppose that $a\in\mathbb R$ and, for each $b>0$, a real finite or countable list $(\lambda_i(b))$ satisfy $$\sum_i|\lambda_i(b)|^4<\infty,\qquad
 Z_L(b):=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_L+aLI)}=\sum_i|\lambda_i(b)|^L
 \quad(L\ge4\text{ even}).$$ Define $S,T$ by <a href="#eq:purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:purities">[eq:purities]</a> and $G$ by <a href="#eq:bootstrap-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-coefficient">[eq:bootstrap-coefficient]</a>. Let $n_0\ge4$ be even, $b_0>0$, and let $u_0,C$ satisfy $$\label{eq:bootstrap-hypotheses}
 0<u_0<\frac16,\qquad G(u_0)\le C,\qquad
 r:=Cu_0<1,\qquad C(1-3u_0)>3.$$ Suppose $$\label{eq:bootstrap-seeds}
 S(n_0,b_0)\ge1-u_0,\qquad T(2n_0,b_0)\ge1-u_0.$$ For $j\ge0$, set <a id="eq:dyadic-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-parameters}
 n_j=2^j n_0,\qquad b_j=2^j b_0,\qquad
 u_j=C^{-1}r^{2^j}.$$ Then $$\label{eq:dyadic-purities}
 S(n_j,b_j)\ge1-u_j,\qquad T(2n_j,b_j)\ge1-u_j.$$ For every even $L\ge n_0$, the physical ground state is unique, and the gap above it satisfies $$\label{eq:bootstrap-gap}
 \gamma_L>\frac{\log(1/r)}{b_0}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-bootstrap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-bootstrap</summary>

*Proof.* *Doubling.* The definition gives $u_{j+1}=Cu_j^2$ and $0<u_{j+1}<u_j\le u_0$. Suppose both defects at $(n_j,b_j)$ are at most $u=u_j$. The first bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:rounded-update" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounded-update">7</a> is at most $$f\bigl(1-(1-u)^3\bigr)=G(u)u^2\le Cu^2=u_{j+1}\le u.$$ Use $p_+=u_{j+1}$ in the second update. Its input is at most $1-(1-u)^3$, so its output is also at most $Cu^2=u_{j+1}$. Induction proves <a href="#eq:dyadic-purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-purities">[eq:dyadic-purities]</a>.

*Intermediate even lengths.* For any nonnegative list $(a_i)$ with finite positive $p$th-power sum and $q\ge p>0$, normalization gives $$\sum_i a_i^q\le\left(\sum_i a_i^p\right)^{q/p}.$$ Indeed the normalized $p$th powers are probabilities, whose $(q/p)$th powers sum to at most one. Apply this to the absolute spatial eigenvalues at each of the two temperatures. Whenever $n\le L\le2n$ and both lengths are even, $$Z_L(b)\le Z_n(b)^{L/n},\qquad
 Z_L(2b)\ge Z_{2n}(2b)^{L/(2n)}.$$ Combining them yields the interpolation inequality <a id="eq:purity-interpolation"></a>$$\label{eq:purity-interpolation}
 T(L,b)\ge
 \left[T(2n,b)S(n,b)^2\right]^{L/(2n)}.$$ The bracket lies in $(0,1]$ and the exponent lies in $[1/2,1]$. For $n=n_j$ and $b=b_j$, we therefore have <a id="eq:intermediate-purity"></a>$$\label{eq:intermediate-purity}
 T(L,b_j)\ge(1-u_j)^3\ge1-3u_j>\frac12
 \qquad(n_j\le L\le2n_j,\ L\text{ even}).$$

*The physical spectrum.* The largest weight in <a href="#eq:gibbs-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gibbs-weights">[eq:gibbs-weights]</a> is at least its purity, since $\sum_k w_k^2\le(\max_k w_k)\sum_k w_k$. It is consequently greater than $1/2$. These weights count all physical eigenvectors; two ground-state eigenvectors would have two equal maximal weights, which is impossible. Thus the ground state is unique.

Because $H_L$ is non-scalar and self-adjoint in finite dimension, a next distinct energy exists. Let $w_0$ be the ground-state weight and let $w_1$ be the weight of an eigenvector at that next energy. Then <a id="eq:gap-ratio"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 e^{-b_j\gamma_L}
 &=\frac{w_1}{w_0}
 \le\frac{1-w_0}{w_0}
 \le\frac{3u_j}{1-3u_j}\notag\\
 &\le\frac{3}{C(1-3u_0)}r^{2^j}
 <r^{2^j}.
 \label{eq:gap-ratio}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the shift $aL$ cancels, and the final strict inequality follows from <a href="#eq:bootstrap-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-hypotheses">[eq:bootstrap-hypotheses]</a>. Taking logarithms and using $b_j=2^j b_0$ proves <a href="#eq:bootstrap-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-gap">[eq:bootstrap-gap]</a> throughout the interval $n_j\le L\le2n_j$. These dyadic intervals cover every even $L\ge n_0$. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:bootstrap">1</a> summarizes the two operations in the proof: doubling advances the pair of purity estimates, and interpolation fills the interval of physical lengths controlled at each temperature.

<figure id="fig:bootstrap">

<figcaption>The bootstrap, shown schematically at three dyadic scales. Dashed arrows double the starting length and inverse temperature. At each scale, interpolation controls the physical purity for every even length on the solid interval. The intervals cover all larger even lengths.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-bootstrap"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:bootstrap`)

   {\draw (\x,-0.5)--(\x,-0.6);
    \node[below] at (\x,-0.6) {$\lab$};}

   {\node[left] at (-0.4,\y) {$\lab$};}

Horizontal axis has tick marks labeled $n_0$, $2n_0$, $4n_0$, $8n_0$; vertical axis has tick marks labeled $b_0$, $2b_0$, $4b_0$. Dashed arrows point diagonally upward and to the right, doubling the starting length and inverse temperature between successive scales.
