<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The two parameter choices

We will establish the initial inequalities <a href="#eq:bootstrap-seeds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-seeds">[eq:bootstrap-seeds]</a> for the following two rows: <a id="eq:bootstrap-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:bootstrap-parameters}
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c}
 n_0&b_0&u_0&C&r=Cu_0\\ \hline
 60&105/4&1/8&79/10&79/80\\
 2304&784&1/100&5&1/20
\end{array}$$ The scalar hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bootstrap">8</a> can already be checked. For the first row, $$G(1/8)=\frac{913952}{117649}<\frac{79}{10},\qquad
 \frac{3}{C(1-3u_0)}=\frac{48}{79}<1.$$ For the second, $1-(1-u)^3\le3u$ gives, when $0<u\le1/100$, $$G(u)\le\frac{9}{2(1-3u)^2}
 \le\frac{45000}{9409}<5,\qquad
 \frac{3}{C(1-3u_0)}=\frac{60}{97}<1.$$ Thus the two rows give respectively the bounds $(4/105)\log(80/79)$ and $(\log20)/784$ in <a href="#eq:bootstrap-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap-gap">[eq:bootstrap-gap]</a>, once their initial purities are proved. The second row will follow from six applications of Lemma <a href="#lem:rounded-update" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounded-update">7</a> to bounds based at $(36,49/4)$. The remaining task is therefore finite: establish the two sets of initial inequalities without losing the margins needed in these updates.

<a id="sec:initialization"></a>
### Two finite initializations

The doubling argument requires a spatial purity at length $n$ and a physical purity at length $2n$, at the same inverse temperature. We establish two such pairs. The first begins at length $60$; the second begins at length $2304$ and gives the stronger eventual gap. In both cases the inputs are a few short-chain partition functions and one periodic trial vector. This section explains how those finite inputs imply the required purities.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Thermal data and elementary moment bounds

<a id="eq:sector-inversion"></a>
We use the sectors from Lemma <a href="#lem:sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sectors">5</a>: $J_n=I_n+2O_n$ is the $D_2$-invariant moment, $I_n$ is the moment fixed also by the cyclic rotation, and $N_n$ is the moment in one nontrivial $D_2$ sector. Each $O_n$ occurs twice and each $N_n$ occurs three times in the full trace. In particular, $$\label{eq:sector-inversion}
\begin{aligned}
 J_n&=\frac{Z_n+3Z_n(P)}4,&
 N_n&=\frac{Z_n-Z_n(P)}4,\\
 I_n&=\frac{Z_n+3Z_n(P)+8Z_n(C)}{12},&
 O_n&=\frac{Z_n+3Z_n(P)-4Z_n(C)}{12}.
\end{aligned}$$ The inverse temperature is common to every term of each identity.

<a id="stmt-lem-thermal-inputs"></a>
<div id="lem:thermal-inputs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Finite thermal inputs). *For $a=700741/500000$, the following tables give centers for the shifted partition functions $Z_n(b,g)$ in units $10^{-6}$. The absolute errors are less than $10^{-5}$ at $b=21/2$ and less than $30\cdot10^{-6}$ at $b=49/4$. $$\begin{array}{c|rrr}
 &\multicolumn{3}{c}{b=21/2}\\
 n&g=1&g=P&g=C\\ \hline
 6&8945303&346734&939987\\
 8&3741281&567167&984108\\
 10&2327547&721090&1002613
\end{array}
\qquad
\begin{array}{c|rr}
 &\multicolumn{2}{c}{b=49/4}\\
 n&g=1&g=P\\ \hline
 4&124885379&65757\\
 5&2925&2960742\\
 6&12870614&286599\\
 7&54069&1920414\\
 8&4639283&510140\\
 9&195455&1514846\\
 10&2654823&675419\\
 11&377629&1314544\\
 12&1900547&787405
\end{array}$$*

</div>

<a id="eq:trial-thermal-consequence"></a>
Appendices <a href="#sec:thermal120" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:thermal120">7</a> and <a href="#sec:thermal72" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:thermal72">8</a> prove the respective tables by rational approximations to the physical matrix exponentials, with explicit error bounds. Lemma <a href="#lem:trial-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trial-inputs">17</a> supplies the other finite input: $$\label{eq:trial-thermal-consequence}
 E_0(72)<-72a,\qquad E_0(120)<-120a.$$ Consequently $Z_{72}(b)>1$ and $Z_{120}(b)>1$ for every $b>0$: in each case one Boltzmann factor already exceeds one.

Here are two elementary ways to extract spectral information from the tables.

<a id="stmt-lem-cappedmass"></a>
<div id="lem:cappedmass" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:capped-function"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Capped masses). *Let $(t_i)$ be a finite or countable family with $0\le t_i\le h$ and $\sum_i t_i\le m$, where $h>0$ and $m\ge0$. For $r\ge1$, put $$\label{eq:capped-function}
 \mathcal C(m,h,r)=q h^r+(m-qh)^r,\qquad q=\lfloor m/h\rfloor.$$ Then $\sum_i t_i^r\le\mathcal C(m,h,r)$. Without the individual cap, $\sum_i t_i^r\le m^r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cappedmass"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cappedmass</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite family at fixed total mass, transfer mass from the smaller of two coordinates in $(0,h)$ to the larger until one reaches $0$ or $h$. Convexity shows that the sum of $r$th powers does not decrease. Repetition leaves at most one coordinate in $(0,h)$, giving <a href="#eq:capped-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capped-function">[eq:capped-function]</a> at the actual total mass. That expression is continuous and nondecreasing in the total mass, so it is bounded by its value at $m$. Apply this argument to finite subfamilies and take their increasing union for a countable family. The uncapped assertion follows directly from $t_i^r\le t_i(\sum_jt_j)^{r-1}$ when the total mass is positive; the zero case is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-polynomial-filter"></a>
<div id="lem:polynomial-filter" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Polynomial filter). *Let $\epsilon\ge0$, $v>0$, and let $(\lambda_i)$ be a real eigenvalue list with moments $M_j=\sum_i\lambda_i^j$, and suppose $|M_j-c_j|\le\epsilon$ for the indices occurring in a polynomial $F(x)=\sum_j f_jx^j$. Assume these moments converge absolutely and $F\ge0$ on $\mathbb R$. If $$B=\sum_j f_jc_j+\epsilon\sum_j|f_j|
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad F(x)>B\ \hbox{for }|x|\ge v,$$ then every $|\lambda_i|<v$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-filter"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-filter</summary>

*Proof.* Absolute convergence gives $\sum_iF(\lambda_i)\le B$. Since every summand is nonnegative, one eigenvalue with $|\lambda_i|\ge v$ would contradict this bound. ◻

</details>

All moments used below converge absolutely by Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>. The coefficient sums in <a href="#eq:sector-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-inversion">[eq:sector-inversion]</a> are at most one in absolute value, so the thermal error bound also bounds each sector-moment error. This observation includes the odd moments used in the second initialization; their signs are retained in the polynomial filter.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The initialization at length sixty

<a id="stmt-prop-seed60"></a>
<div id="prop:seed60" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *At inverse temperature $b=105/4$, $$S(60,b)>\frac78,\qquad T(120,b)>\frac78.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-seed60"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-seed60</summary>

*Proof.* We first bound sector moments and then increase the inverse temperature. The trial energy forces a dominant spatial weight, giving the spatial purity; its physical Boltzmann factor will give the physical purity separately.

Begin at the lower inverse temperature $b_0=21/2$. From the $b_0=21/2$ table in Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a> and <a href="#eq:sector-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-inversion">[eq:sector-inversion]</a>, upper bounds for $I_{10},O_{10},N_{10}$ are, respectively, $$m_I=\frac{1042655}{10^6},\qquad
 m_O=\frac{40041}{10^6},\qquad
 m_N=\frac{401625}{10^6}.$$ For clarity, their margins over the sector centers plus $10^{-5}$ are $19/(12\cdot10^6)$, $7/(12\cdot10^6)$ and $3/(4\cdot10^6)$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-filter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-filter">11</a> to $$F_I(x)=x^6(-2+10x^2)^2,\qquad
 F_N(x)=x^6(-25+100x^2)^2.$$ For a filter $x^6(c_0+c_1x^2)^2$, its summed upper bound is the centered combination of moments $6,8,10$ with coefficients $c_0^2,2c_0c_1,c_1^2$, plus $10^{-5}(|c_0|+|c_1|)^2$. Direct rational evaluation gives $$|\lambda|<U:=\frac{100245}{100000}\quad\hbox{in }I,
 \qquad
 |\lambda|<V:=\frac{88643}{100000}\quad\hbox{in }N.$$ Indeed the filter value at the indicated endpoint exceeds its summed upper bound by more than $0.0301$ for $I$ and $0.3463$ for $N$. Each filter is strictly increasing with $|x|$ beyond its endpoint: there $c_1>0$ and $c_0+c_1x^2>0$.

<a id="eq:short-moment-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:seed60-scalars"></a>
The rational inequalities $U^{10}<m_I<2U^{10}$ and $V^{10}<m_N<2V^{10}$ allow the two-piece form of Lemma <a href="#lem:cappedmass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cappedmass">10</a>. For $k=30,120$ define $$A_k=U^k+(m_I-U^{10})^{k/10},\qquad
 B_k=m_O^{k/10},\qquad
 C_k=V^k+(m_N-V^{10})^{k/10}.$$ They bound $I_k,O_k,N_k$, respectively. Because these are even moments, all are nonnegative, and hence $$\label{eq:short-moment-upper}
 Z_k(b_0)\le A_k+2B_k+3C_k,\qquad
 Z_k(b_0,P),\ Z_k(b_0,C)\le A_k+2B_k.$$ Put $m=A_{30}+2B_{30}+3C_{30}$, $p=A_{30}+2B_{30}$, and $q=A_{120}+2B_{120}+3C_{120}$. With $$D=\frac{1450}{1000},\quad E=\frac{1202}{1000},\quad
 \tau=\frac{1225}{1000},\quad\tau_0=\frac{1264}{1000},\quad
 R_q=\frac{684}{10000},$$ the remaining scalar estimates are $$\label{eq:seed60-scalars}
\begin{gathered}
 m^5<D^2,\qquad p^5<E^2,\qquad
 q\ge1,\qquad (q-1)^5<R_q^2,\\
 \frac{D+11E}{12}\le\tau,\qquad
 \frac{D+3E}{4}\le\tau_0.
\end{gathered}$$ For example, the positive margins in the first, second and fourth strict inequalities exceed $0.001068$, $0.000433$ and $0.0000472$, respectively. Every number in these comparisons is rational and is specified above.

We now increase the inverse temperature from $b_0$ to $b=(5/2)b_0$. For positive Boltzmann factors $z_i$, $\sum_i z_i^{5/2}\le(\sum_i z_i)^{5/2}$. Thus <a href="#eq:short-moment-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:short-moment-upper">[eq:short-moment-upper]</a>–<a href="#eq:seed60-scalars" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed60-scalars">[eq:seed60-scalars]</a> imply $$Z_{30}(b)\le D,\qquad Z_{30}(b,P),Z_{30}(b,C)\le E.$$ By <a href="#eq:sector-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-inversion">[eq:sector-inversion]</a>, the $I$ and $J$ portions of the thirtieth spatial moment are at most $\tau$ and $\tau_0$.

Let $w_i=|\lambda_i(X_b)|^{30}$. Then $\sum_iw_i\le D$, whereas <a href="#eq:trial-thermal-consequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-thermal-consequence">[eq:trial-thermal-consequence]</a> gives $\sum_iw_i^4=Z_{120}(b)>1$. Since $D(0.88)^3<1$, some weight $w_0>0.88$ exists. It is unique because $2(0.88)>D$. It belongs to $I$: a weight in $O$ would occur at least twice, and one in $N$ at least three times. These statements count all multiplicities, including equal moduli of opposite-sign spatial eigenvalues.

To sharpen the lower bound on $w_0$, suppose $0\le t\le0.998$ and $w_0\ge t$. Denote the remaining $I$ mass by $x$, the full $O$-pair mass by $y$, and the full $N$-triple mass by $z$. Their squared weights sum to at most $x^2+y^2/2+z^2/3$, and $$0\le x\le\tau-t,\qquad 0\le y,\qquad
 x+y\le\tau_0-t,\qquad z\le D-t-x-y.$$ The divisors $2$ and $3$ use the identical weight lists in the repeated sectors. Since $D>\tau_0$, the last upper bound is nonnegative on the entire displayed polygon. The convex quadratic $x^2+y^2/2+(D-t-x-y)^2/3$ has its maximum at one of its four vertices. Write <a id="eq:residual-R"></a>$$\label{eq:residual-R}
 R(t)=\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal V_t}
 \left(x^2+\frac{y^2}{2}+\frac{(D-t-x-y)^2}{3}\right),$$ where $$\mathcal V_t=\{(0,0),(\tau-t,0),
 (\tau-t,\tau_0-\tau),(0,\tau_0-t)\}.$$ Thus the remaining squared weights sum to at most $R(t)$, and their fourth powers sum to at most $R(t)^2$. Exact evaluation gives $$\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 t&0.88&0.99&0.998\\ \hline
 R(t)&0.1359&0.0721&0.068404
\end{array}$$ and $$1-0.99^4-R(0.88)^2=0.02093518,\qquad
 1-0.998^4-R(0.99)^2=0.002777621984.$$ If $w_0\le0.99$, the first equality would contradict $\sum_iw_i^4>1$; the second then excludes $w_0\le0.998$. Keeping the dominant contribution in the fourth moment yields $$S(60,b)=\frac{\sum_iw_i^4}{(\sum_iw_i^2)^2}
 \ge\left(1+\frac{R(0.998)}{0.998^2}\right)^{-2}
 >0.8756>\frac78.$$

For the physical purity at length $120$, return briefly to temperature $b_0$. Its largest Boltzmann factor $z_0$ exceeds one by the trial bound, while the sum of all factors is at most $q$. After raising the factors to the power $5/2$, the ratio of the remaining mass to $z_0^{5/2}$ is at most $(q-1)^{5/2}<R_q$. Therefore $$T(120,b)\ge(1+R_q)^{-2}>0.8760>\frac78.$$ This last step concerns physical Boltzmann factors, separately from the spatial weights used to bound $S$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The initialization giving the stronger gap

<a id="stmt-prop-seed2304"></a>
<div id="prop:seed2304" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *At inverse temperature $b=784$, $$1-S(2304,b)\le\frac{84513}{10^7}<\frac1{100},\qquad
 1-T(4608,b)\le\frac{27493}{5\cdot10^6}<\frac1{100}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-seed2304"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-seed2304</summary>

*Proof.* We isolate one large eigenvalue in $J$ and bound the remaining moments. This gives a pair of initial purity defects at length $36$; six rounded updates then reach length $2304$.

Begin at $b_0=49/4$. The $b_0=49/4$ table gives centers for $J_n,N_n$ through <a href="#eq:sector-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-inversion">[eq:sector-inversion]</a>, with error at most $\epsilon=30\cdot10^{-6}$. Use the two filters $$F_J(x)=x^4(7+17x-280x^2-185x^3+1000x^4)^2,
 \qquad F_N(x)=x^4(12-394x^2+1000x^4)^2.$$ Their degree is $12$, so the table supplies every required moment. The summed upper bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-filter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-filter">11</a> are exactly $$B_J=318604.54848175,\qquad B_N=48169.880699.$$ Set $v_J=100139/100000$ and $v_N=872/1000$. Direct rational comparisons give $$F_J(v_J)-B_J>180,\quad F_J(-v_J)-B_J>496888,\quad
 F_N(\pm v_N)-B_N>654.$$ To check the whole exterior rays without further numerical tests, write $Q(x)=7+17x-280x^2-185x^3+1000x^4$. For $y\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 Q(1+y)&=559+2902y+5165y^2+3815y^3+1000y^4,\\
 Q(-1-y)&=895+3978y+6275y^2+4185y^3+1000y^4.
\end{aligned}$$ Both are positive and increasing. The polynomial $12-394r^2+1000r^4$ is likewise positive and increasing for $r\ge v_N$: its value at $v_N$ is positive and its derivative is $4r(1000r^2-197)>0$. Thus the filters increase along the requisite rays, and every eigenvalue has modulus less than $v_J$ in $J$ and less than $v_N$ in $N$.

<a id="eq:seed72-tail"></a>
The twelfth-moment upper bounds are $$m_J=1.0657205,\qquad m_N=0.2783155.$$ Put $\ell=999/1000$ and, for $k=36,72$, define $$\label{eq:seed72-tail}
 B(k)=3\mathcal C(m_N,v_N^{12},k/12),\qquad
 R_A(k)=B(k)+(m_J-\ell^{12})^{k/12}.$$ Here $m_J>\ell^{12}$. If all eigenvalues in $J$ had modulus at most $\ell$, Lemma <a href="#lem:cappedmass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cappedmass">10</a> would imply $$Z_{72}(b_0)\le\mathcal C(m_J,\ell^{12},6)+B(72)<1,$$ where the strict margin to one exceeds $0.0693$. The trial inequality contradicts this. Choose an eigenvalue $\lambda_0$ in $J$ with $|\lambda_0|>\ell$. Removing it leaves a twelfth-moment mass at most $m_J-\ell^{12}$ in $J$. The uncapped bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:cappedmass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cappedmass">10</a> and the three $N$ sectors show that $R_A(k)$ bounds the remaining full $k$th moment.

<a id="eq:seed72-pq"></a>
It follows that $$\label{eq:seed72-pq}
\begin{aligned}
 1-S(36,b_0)&\le p_0:=1-\left(1+\frac{R_A(36)}{\ell^{36}}\right)^{-2},\\
 1-T(72,b_0)&\le q_0:=1-\left(v_J^{72}+R_A(72)\right)^{-2}.
\end{aligned}$$ For the first estimate retain $\lambda_0^{72}$ in the numerator; for the second use $Z_{72}(2b_0)>1$ and $Z_{72}(b_0)\le v_J^{72}+R_A(72)$. Numerically $p_0<0.047916$ and $q_0<0.181521$, but the following updates use their exact rational definitions in <a href="#eq:seed72-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed72-tail">[eq:seed72-tail]</a>–<a href="#eq:seed72-pq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed72-pq">[eq:seed72-pq]</a>.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:rounded-update" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounded-update">7</a> six times, rounding each new defect upward to a multiple of $10^{-7}$ and using the rounded spatial defect in the physical update. For completeness, with $f(u)=u^2/[2(1-u)^2]$ and $\lceil x\rceil_7=10^{-7}\lceil10^7x\rceil$, the scalar updates are $$p_{j+1}=\left\lceil f\bigl(1-(1-p_j)^2(1-q_j)\bigr)\right\rceil_7,
 \qquad
 q_{j+1}=\left\lceil f\bigl(1-(1-q_j)^2(1-p_{j+1})\bigr)\right\rceil_7.$$ Exact rational evaluation gives $$\begin{array}{c|cc}
 j&p_j&q_j\\ \hline
 1&151249/2500000&433453/2500000\\
 2&343303/5000000&1632381/10000000\\
 3&44601/625000&361773/2500000\\
 4&632939/10000000&1055161/10000000\\
 5&375793/10000000&445941/10000000\\
 6&84513/10000000&27493/5000000
\end{array}$$ Every update is in $[0,1)$, where $f$ is increasing, so the upward rounding preserves valid bounds. Both the length and inverse temperature have multiplied by $2^6$: $36\cdot64=2304$ and $(49/4)\cdot64=784$. The last row proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A periodic interface for boundary estimates

The boundary-field companion (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BoundaryCompanion](#ref-BoundaryCompanion)] uses the intermediate spatial information in the preceding proof, rather than only the final periodic gap. We state that information separately so that its hypotheses and finite inputs can be invoked together.

<a id="stmt-cor-boundary-inputs"></a>
<div id="cor:boundary-inputs" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14** (Periodic inputs for boundary estimates). *Let $X_b$ be the spatial transfer operator of Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a>, with the shift $a=700741/500000$, and put $$\begin{gathered}
 b_0=49/4,\qquad b_1=49/2,\\
 \ell=999/1000,\qquad U=100139/100000,\qquad V=872/1000,\\
 m_J=1.0657205,\qquad m_N=.2783155.
 \end{gathered}$$ At $b=b_0$, the full eigenvalue list, counted with multiplicity, is the $D_2$-invariant list $J$ together with three identical lists $N$. Every eigenvalue in $J$ has modulus less than $U$, and the sum of the twelfth powers of all eigenvalues in $J$ is at most $m_J$. Every eigenvalue in each $N$ list has modulus less than $V$, and that list’s twelfth powers sum to at most $m_N$. There is an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\ell$. Moreover, $$1-S(72,b_1)\le\frac{151249}{2500000}.$$ For $n=72$ and $n=120$, one has $E_0(n)+na<0$; hence a physical ground Boltzmann factor in $Z_n(b)$ exceeds one for every $b>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-boundary-inputs"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-boundary-inputs</summary>

*Proof.* The decomposition is Lemma <a href="#lem:sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sectors">5</a>, with $J=I+2O$. The filters, twelfth-moment bounds and dominant eigenvalue are proved at the start of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:seed2304" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:seed2304">13</a>. The first row of its rounded update table gives the displayed bound at $(72,49/2)$. The variational assertion is Lemma <a href="#lem:trial-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trial-inputs">17</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Proof of the gap theorem

We now assemble the two initializations through the same purity theorem. The thermal and trial inputs used in Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">5</a> are proved in Appendices <a href="#sec:thermal120" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:thermal120">7</a>–<a href="#sec:trials" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trials">9</a>. Those finite proofs are part of the argument.

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-boundary-inputs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-boundary-inputs</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transfer">4</a> identifies the physical partition functions with moments of the self-adjoint spatial transfer operator. They therefore satisfy the hypotheses used to define both purities in Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">4</a>. The Hamiltonians are self-adjoint and non-scalar, as noted after <a href="#eq:main-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-gap">[eq:main-gap]</a>.

Proposition <a href="#prop:seed60" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:seed60">12</a> supplies $$S(60,105/4)>7/8,\qquad T(120,105/4)>7/8.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bootstrap">8</a> with $$(n_0,b_0,u_0,C)=(60,105/4,1/8,79/10).$$ Its parameter inequalities are verified in Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">4</a>, and $Cu_0=79/80$. It follows that the ground state is unique and $\gamma_L>\frac4{105}\log(80/79)$ for every even $L\ge60$.

Proposition <a href="#prop:seed2304" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:seed2304">13</a> supplies both purity defects at most $1/100$ at the pair based at $(n_0,b_0)=(2304,784)$. Apply the same theorem with $u_0=1/100$ and $C=5$. Since $Cu_0=1/20$, it gives $\gamma_L>\log(20)/784$ for every even $L\ge2304$. Taking the limit inferior over even lengths proves the stated bound on $\Delta_1$. Finally, Lemma <a href="#lem:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:representation">2</a> transfers the spectral conclusions to the other spin-one matrix realization by simultaneous similarity and its tensor powers. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### An infinite-volume consequence

The finite-volume estimate also controls local excitations in a thermodynamic limit. We use the standard limiting argument recalled in (Tasaki 2025, arXiv v4, End Matter, Definition 11 and Theorem 12) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)]. A local operator on the spin chain $\mathbb Z$ acts nontrivially on only finitely many sites. A state $\omega$ is a linear functional on these operators with $\omega(I)=1$ and $\omega(A^*A)\ge0$ for every local operator $A$. Define the local commutator $$[H,A]=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}
 [\mathbf S_j\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1},A].$$ Only finitely many summands are nonzero.

<a id="stmt-cor-local-limit"></a>
<div id="cor:local-limit" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:local-limit-gap"></a>
**Corollary 15**. *For the self-adjoint Hamiltonians <a href="#eq:main-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-H">[eq:main-H]</a>, the normalized ground states of the even periodic rings have a subsequence, as $L\to\infty$, converging on every local operator to a state $\omega$. Every such limit satisfies $$\label{eq:local-limit-gap}
 \omega(A^*[H,A])\ge\frac{\log20}{784}
 \bigl(\omega(A^*A)-|\omega(A)|^2\bigr)$$ for every local operator $A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-local-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-local-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Place the ring of even length $L$ on $\{-L/2,\ldots,L/2-1\}$, with its closing bond at the endpoints. Compactness of the density matrices on each finite interval and a diagonal subsequence give convergence on all local operators. The compatible limits define a positive normalized functional $\omega$. For $L\ge2304$, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a unique ground state, with normalized vector $\psi_L$, and a gap greater than $\delta=\log(20)/784$. Writing $\omega_L(A)=\langle\psi_L,A\psi_L\rangle$, the spectral inequality on the orthogonal complement of $\psi_L$ gives $$\omega_L(A^*[H_L,A])\ge\delta
 \bigl(\omega_L(A^*A)-|\omega_L(A)|^2\bigr).$$ For fixed local $A$ and sufficiently large $L$, the closing bond commutes with $A$ and $[H_L,A]=[H,A]$. Passing to the local limit proves the claim. ◻

</details>

In particular, $\omega$ is a ground state: no local perturbation lowers its energy. For $\omega(A)=0$, inequality <a href="#eq:local-limit-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-limit-gap">[eq:local-limit-gap]</a> is the locally unique gapped property of (Tasaki 2025, arXiv v4, Definition 11) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)], with gap at least $\log(20)/784$. The argument does not identify the limits of different subsequences or establish uniqueness among all infinite-volume ground states.

<a id="sec:thermal120"></a>
### Thermal calculation at inverse temperature $21/2$

We prove the $b=21/2$ table in Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a>. The calculation uses the actual physical Hamiltonians at lengths $n=6,8,10$, with twists $1,P,C$. All arithmetic below is integral or rational; in the cyclic case complex numbers are represented in $\mathbb Z[\omega]$, where $\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The finite matrices and their columns

Fix $b=21/2$ and one of these lengths, and write $H=H_n(g)$. After the global rotations in Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">2</a>, the three twists have angles $\theta=0,\pi,2\pi/3$ about the weight-basis axis. Set <a id="eq:thermal120-matrices"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal120-matrices}
 W=I-\frac{H_n(g)+(3n/2)I}{16},\qquad B=16W.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:energy">3</a> gives $\left\lVert W\right\rVert\le1$ and $\left\lVert B\right\rVert\le16$. In a fixed total-weight sector, the rows and columns are words $x\in\{-1,0,1\}^n$ with fixed $\sum_jx_j$. The diagonal of $B$ is $$B_{x,x}=16-\frac{3n}{2}-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x_{j-1}x_j.$$ For each $j$, put $k=j+1\pmod n$. If $y_j=x_j-\delta$, $y_k=x_k+\delta$, $\delta\in\{-1,1\}$, and all letters remain in $\{-1,0,1\}$, the corresponding entry is $-1$, except at $j=n-1,k=0$, where it is $-e^{i\theta\delta}$. This is exactly the physical seam phase in <a href="#eq:seam-phase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seam-phase">[eq:seam-phase]</a>. For the cyclic twist its action on a coordinate $u+v\omega$ uses <a id="eq:thermal120-phases"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal120-phases}
 -\omega(u+v\omega)=v+(v-u)\omega,
 \qquad
 -\omega^{-1}(u+v\omega)=(u-v)+u\omega.$$ Thus matrix-vector multiplication requires only integer additions and multiplications in both coordinates.

Only some columns need to be computed. To justify the reduction, let $s(x)=(x_{n-1},x_0,\ldots,x_{n-2})$. The seam formula gives $$H_{s(x),s(y)}
 =e^{i\theta(y_{n-1}-x_{n-1})}H_{x,y}.$$ Consequently the monomial unitary $|x\rangle\mapsto e^{-i\theta x_{n-1}}|s(x)\rangle$ commutes with $H$. Reversal of the word takes $H$ to its complex conjugate, as does negation of every letter. Composing either permutation with complex conjugation gives an antiunitary symmetry. Each of these symmetries preserves the norm of the corresponding column of any real exponential of $H$.

In every sector of nonnegative total weight, select the lexicographically least word in each orbit under rotations and reversal. Give it the orbit size as its multiplicity, doubled for strictly positive total weight to include the opposite sector. The multiplicities then sum to $3^n$ and reproduce the full Frobenius norm of the exact exponential. No symmetry of the rounded columns is required: we compare each computed representative with its own exact column and repeat that comparison with the same multiplicity.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A rational polynomial and its error

Write $$E=e^{84(W-I)}=e^{-(b/2)(H_n(g)+(3n/2)I)},
 \qquad x_n=(3/2-a)bn.$$ The desired trace is <a id="eq:thermal120-frobenius"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal120-frobenius}
 Z_n(b,g)=e^{x_n}\left\lVert E\right\rVert_F^2,$$ where $\left\lVert \cdot\right\rVert_F$ is the Frobenius norm. For $0\le j\le J=256$, define the rational probabilities <a id="eq:thermal120-probabilities"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal120-probabilities}
 p_j=\frac{84^j/j!}{\sum_{l=0}^{256}84^l/l!},
 \qquad P(W)=\sum_{j=0}^{256}p_jW^j.$$ These are Poisson probabilities of mean $84$, conditioned on values at most $256$. For a Poisson variable $V$ with that mean, $$\Pr(V>256)\le\frac{\mathbb E(2^V)}{2^{257}}
 =\frac{e^{84}}{2^{257}}
 <\frac{27^{28}}{2^{257}}<2^{-117}.$$ The conditioning estimate is the coefficient argument of Fox–Glynn (Fox and Glynn 1988, Proposition 1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[FoxGlynn1988](#ref-FoxGlynn1988)], applied here to powers of a contraction. Since $\left\lVert W^j\right\rVert\le1$, conditioning changes the operator average by at most twice this probability. Hence <a id="eq:thermal120-poisson-error"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal120-poisson-error}
 \left\lVert P(W)-E\right\rVert<2^{-116}.$$

<a id="eq:thermal120-horner"></a>
<a id="eq:thermal120-column-error"></a>
Put $Q=2^{56}$ and $q_j=\lfloor Qp_j\rfloor$. For a representative column $r$, start at zero and apply, for $j=256,\ldots,0$, $$\label{eq:thermal120-horner}
 z\longleftarrow\operatorname{fl}(Bz/16)+q_j e_r.$$ For real coordinates, $\operatorname{fl}$ is the ordinary floor; for $u+v\omega$ it floors $u$ and $v$ separately. The ideal suffix at step $j$ is $Q\sum_{l=j}^{256}p_lW^{l-j}e_r$, whose norm is at most $Q$. In a sector of dimension $d_0$, the Euclidean norm of the error from a coordinatewise floor is less than $2\sqrt{d_0}$, and the coefficient floor adds at most one. Contraction of $W$ therefore bounds the error at every stage by $$\label{eq:thermal120-column-error}
 257(2\sqrt{3^n}+1)\le125159.$$ The zero coefficients above $q_{172}$ may be omitted: the computed vector is still zero there, and <a href="#eq:thermal120-column-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-column-error">[eq:thermal120-column-error]</a> continues to count all $257$ possible rounding steps.

<a id="eq:thermal120-scaled-error"></a>
The norm of the repeated computed-column list, divided by $Q$ and multiplied by $e^{x_n/2}$, differs from $\sqrt{Z_n(b,g)}$ by at most $$\label{eq:thermal120-scaled-error}
 e^{x_n/2}\sqrt{3^n}
 \left(\frac{257(2\sqrt{3^n}+1)}Q+2^{-116}\right)
 <4\cdot10^{-7}.$$ Indeed $\sqrt{3^n}\le243$, $x_n/2<6$, and $e^{x_n/2}<3^6$. The resulting rational upper bound $3^6\,243(125159/2^{56}+2^{-116})$ is less than $4\cdot10^{-7}$. This comparison includes both polynomial truncation and integer rounding.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Exact evaluation by packed integers

For efficiency, the calculation performs <a href="#eq:thermal120-horner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-horner">[eq:thermal120-horner]</a> on batches of at most $192$ columns. A list of signed integer digits $y_0,\ldots,y_{w-1}$ is stored as $$\operatorname{pack}(y)=\sum_{t=0}^{w-1}y_t2^{64t}.$$ Integer linear arithmetic preserves this identity before the floor, even if intermediate carries occur. Arbitrary-precision integers are used throughout.

<a id="eq:thermal120-lane-bound"></a>
Here is why the floor can also be performed on the packed integer. For either coefficient of $u+v\omega$, $$|u|,|v|\le\frac2{\sqrt3}|u+v\omega|\le2|u+v\omega|.$$ By <a href="#eq:thermal120-column-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-column-error">[eq:thermal120-column-error]</a>, every computed column has norm at most $Q+125159$. Thus every coordinate of $Bz$, in either integer component, has magnitude at most $$\label{eq:thermal120-lane-bound}
 32(Q+125159)=2305843009217699040<2^{63}.$$ Let $s_w=\sum_{t=0}^{w-1}2^{64t}$ and define $$\mathrm{bias}=2^{63}s_w,\qquad
 \mathrm{mask}=(2^{60}-1)s_w,\qquad
 \mathrm{bias16}=2^{59}s_w.$$ For $Y=\operatorname{pack}(y)$ satisfying <a href="#eq:thermal120-lane-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-lane-bound">[eq:thermal120-lane-bound]</a>, the operation $$\texttt{(((Y+bias)>{}>4)\&mask)-bias16}$$ equals $\operatorname{pack}((\lfloor y_t/16\rfloor)_{t=0}^{w-1})$. To see this, adding the bias makes every base-$2^{64}$ digit lie in $[0,2^{64})$. The global right shift moves four low bits from each higher lane into the four high bits of the preceding lane. The mask deletes precisely those crossing bits, retaining $\lfloor(y_t+2^{63})/16\rfloor$ in lane $t$. Subtracting $2^{59}$ then gives $\lfloor y_t/16\rfloor$, also for negative $y_t$. This proves by induction that the packed recurrence is exactly <a href="#eq:thermal120-horner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-horner">[eq:thermal120-horner]</a>.

The final coordinates satisfy the smaller bound $2(Q+125159)<2^{63}$, so biasing and unpacking recovers every integer coordinate exactly. If a final coordinate is $u+v\omega$, its squared modulus is $u^2-uv+v^2$. Summing these integers with the representative multiplicities gives an integer $t_{n,g}$, the squared norm of the full repeated-column list in $Q$-units.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The exact totals and trace enclosures

Integer evaluation of the recurrence gives the following totals. The accompanying computation implements the matrix entries, representatives and packed operations specified above.

<div class="center">

| $n$ | $g$ | $t_{n,g}$                        |
|:---:|:---:|:---------------------------------|
|  6  | $1$ | 93637037510293158533967385845216 |
|  6  | $P$ | 3629519998654831111446492683663  |
|  6  | $C$ | 9839536903588146388838689926305  |
|  8  | $1$ | 4947327904487991279560670127283  |
|  8  | $P$ | 749999795795292705866802820621   |
|  8  | $C$ | 1301346729026243689610427021457  |
| 10  | $1$ | 388818111664728958208623998490   |
| 10  | $P$ | 120458568770462253326886628639   |
| 10  | $C$ | 167487042402286114495695779656   |

</div>

<a id="eq:thermal120-rational-enclosure"></a>
To enclose the scalar factor, put $$L_n=\sum_{j=0}^{100}\frac{x_n^j}{j!},\qquad
 U_n=L_n+2\frac{x_n^{101}}{101!}.$$ Since $0<x_n<12$ and successive terms after the first omitted term have ratio at most $x_n/102<1/2$, we have $L_n<e^{x_n}<U_n$. For each center $c_{n,g}$ in the $b=21/2$ table of Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a>, exact rational comparison gives $$\label{eq:thermal120-rational-enclosure}
 c_{n,g}-2\cdot10^{-6}
 <\frac{L_n t_{n,g}}{Q^2}
 <\frac{U_n t_{n,g}}{Q^2}
 <c_{n,g}+2\cdot10^{-6}.$$ Here the centers are interpreted as real numbers, including their $10^{-6}$ scale. These inequalities in particular put every computed scaled norm below $4$. By <a href="#eq:thermal120-scaled-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-scaled-error">[eq:thermal120-scaled-error]</a>, replacing its square by the true trace incurs error less than $(8+4\cdot10^{-7})4\cdot10^{-7}$. Together with <a href="#eq:thermal120-rational-enclosure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal120-rational-enclosure">[eq:thermal120-rational-enclosure]</a>, this yields $$|Z_n(21/2,g)-c_{n,g}|
 <2\cdot10^{-6}+(8+4\cdot10^{-7})4\cdot10^{-7}
 <10^{-5}.$$ This proves the $b=21/2$ table in Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a>.

<a id="sec:thermal72"></a>
### Thermal calculation at inverse temperature $49/4$

We prove the $b=49/4$ table in Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a>. The computation uses integer approximations to columns of a physical matrix exponential. We specify the integer algorithm, bound its error, and give its exact outputs. Throughout this section, $$b=\frac{49}{4},\qquad J=280,\qquad Q=2^{55},\qquad 4\le n\le12.$$ The twists are the identity and a rotation by $\pi$ about the $z$ axis. The latter has the same partition function as $P$, by the global rotation described in Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The physical matrix and its rational approximation

In the weight basis, write a word as $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{n-1})$, with $w_i\in\{-1,0,1\}$. The total weight $\sum_iw_i$ is conserved. In each weight block let $H$ be the restriction of $H_n(g)$, and set <a id="eq:thermal72-matrix"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal72-matrix}
 R=I-\frac{\frac32nI+H}{16},\qquad D=32R.$$ By <a href="#eq:seam-phase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seam-phase">[eq:seam-phase]</a>, $D$ is an integer matrix with diagonal $$D_{w,w}=32-3n-2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_{i-1}w_i.$$ An allowed opposite unit step on the letters at $i-1,i$ has entry $-2$, except that the entry is $+2$ on the twisted seam $i=0$. These formulas specify every matrix entry; indices on words are modulo $n$. The unit step coefficient follows also directly from the two ladder factors $\sqrt2$ and the transverse prefactor $1/2$.

<a id="eq:thermal72-trace"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:energy">3</a> gives $$0\le \frac32nI+H\le\frac52nI,\qquad
 \operatorname{spec}(R)\subseteq[1-5n/32,1]\subseteq[-7/8,1].$$ Thus $R$ is a self-adjoint contraction. Put $$F=\exp\!\left[-\frac b2\left(\frac32nI+H\right)\right]
   =\exp[98(R-I)].$$ We use the same symbols for the direct sums over all weight blocks. On the full physical space, $$\label{eq:thermal72-trace}
 Z_n(b,g)=e^{x_n}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_F^2,\qquad
 x_n=bn\left(\frac32-a\right),$$ where $\left\lVert \cdot\right\rVert_F$ denotes the Frobenius norm and the squared norms of the weight blocks are summed.

<a id="eq:thermal72-coefficients"></a>
Define the rational probabilities $$\label{eq:thermal72-coefficients}
 p_j=\frac{v_j}{\sum_{\ell=0}^Jv_\ell},
 \qquad v_j=98^j\frac{J!}{j!},
 \qquad 0\le j\le J.$$ They are Poisson weights of mean $98$, conditioned to indices at most $J$. Since $\left\lVert R\right\rVert\le1$, discarding the Poisson tail and renormalizing changes the exponential by at most twice the tail probability, by the same coefficient estimate as in Fox–Glynn (Fox and Glynn 1988, Proposition 1) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[FoxGlynn1988](#ref-FoxGlynn1988)]: $$\left\|F-\sum_{j=0}^Jp_jR^j\right\|
 \le 2e^{-98}\sum_{j=281}^{\infty}\frac{98^j}{j!}
 \le2^{-280}e^{98}<2^{-84}<Q^{-1}.$$ For the second inequality, use $2^j\ge2^{281}$ in the tail and $e^{-98}\sum_{j\ge0}196^j/j!=e^{98}$; the next uses $e<4$. The estimate includes the change of normalization.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Integer columns and their error

<a id="eq:thermal72-horner"></a>
For a selected unit basis column $e_r$, start with $X=0$ and perform, in the order $j=J,J-1,\ldots,0$, $$\label{eq:thermal72-horner}
 X\ \longleftarrow\
 \left\lfloor\frac{DX}{32}\right\rfloor+c_je_r,
 \qquad c_j=\lfloor Qp_j\rfloor.$$ The vector floor is coordinatewise. All quantities in this recursion are integers. The coefficients $c_j$ vanish for $j>191$, so those initial zero steps may be omitted without changing $X$.

<a id="eq:thermal72-column-error"></a>
To bound rounding, compare with the ideal suffix $$Y_j=Q\sum_{\ell=j}^Jp_\ell R^{\ell-j}e_r,\qquad \left\lVert Y_j\right\rVert_2\le Q.$$ In a block of dimension $d_0$, one vector floor introduces Euclidean error less than $\sqrt{d_0}$, and one coefficient floor error less than one. The contraction property therefore bounds the accumulated error by $281(\sqrt{d_0}+1)$. Set <a id="eq:thermal72-errors"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal72-errors}
 d_n=\lfloor\sqrt{3^n}\rfloor+1,\qquad
 E_n=281(d_n+1)+1,\qquad \mathcal E_n=d_nE_n.$$ Including the preceding exponential-approximation error, every computed column satisfies $$\label{eq:thermal72-column-error}
 \left\lVert X-QFe_r\right\rVert_2\le E_n.$$ The bound charges all $281$ ideal steps, including the omitted steps whose rational coefficients are nonzero but whose integer floors vanish.

The recursion can be evaluated exactly with signed $64$-bit integers. Indeed every intermediate coordinate has absolute value at most $$Q+281(d_n+1)<2Q,\qquad d_n\le730.$$ The absolute sum of any row of $D$ is at most $$|32-3n|+2n+4n\le76.$$ The first two terms bound the diagonal, and at most two hops per bond give the last term. Consequently every product and every partial sum in a sparse matrix multiplication has absolute value less than $$76\cdot2Q=5476377146882523136<2^{63}-1.$$ Division by $32$ and addition of $c_j\le Q$ also fit; even their conservative absolute bound is $$\frac{76\cdot2Q}{32}+Q=207165582859042816<2^{63}-1.$$ Starting with $X=0$, these bounds first establish exactness of the next integer operation; the contraction estimate then establishes the next coordinate bound. Thus the induction does not assume the absence of overflow that it is proving. All final squares and sums below use arbitrary-precision integers.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Representative columns

Only one column from each of the following word orbits is needed. For positive total weight, use the orbit under cyclic shifts and reversal, and give its representative twice the orbit size. This includes the uncomputed negative-weight block. In the zero-weight block, include digit negation in the orbit itself and use its size without an extra factor two. Choose the lexicographically least word as representative. Set cardinalities handle periodic words and all other nontrivial stabilizers. The multiplicities $\nu_r$ satisfy $$\sum_r\nu_r=3^n.$$

Here are the exact symmetries underlying this reduction. Reversal $w\mapsto(w_{n-1},\ldots,w_0)$ fixes the seam as an unordered bond, and global negation preserves every Hamiltonian entry. For the twisted chain, a bare shift moves the seam. Left rotation by $q$ moves it to $(k-1,k)$, where $k=-q\bmod n$. The diagonal unitary $$G_k|w\rangle=(-1)^{\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}w_j}|w\rangle,\qquad G_0=I,$$ flips the hopping signs exactly at the two boundary bonds of this arc. It restores the original seam. The resulting signed shift commutes with $H$, so exact exponential columns at rotated words have equal norms. For the untwisted chain no sign correction is needed.

<a id="eq:thermal72-total"></a>
<a id="eq:thermal72-frobenius"></a>
Let $X_r$ be the output of <a href="#eq:thermal72-horner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-horner">[eq:thermal72-horner]</a> at representative $r$, and define the integer $$\label{eq:thermal72-total}
 W_{n,g}=\sum_r\nu_r\sum_w X_r(w)^2.$$ Rounding need not preserve the signed symmetries. To compare norms, form abstract direct sums repeating each exact representative column $\nu_r$ times, and do the same for each computed column. The first direct sum has squared norm $Q^2\left\lVert F\right\rVert_F^2$, and the second has squared norm $W_{n,g}$. By <a href="#eq:thermal72-column-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-column-error">[eq:thermal72-column-error]</a>, $$\label{eq:thermal72-frobenius}
 \left|\sqrt{W_{n,g}}-Q\left\lVert F\right\rVert_F\right|
 \le\sqrt{3^n}\,E_n<\mathcal E_n.$$ This proves the full trace estimate using representatives, without requiring their rounded columns to transform equivariantly.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Exact totals and rational enclosures

The integer algorithm <a href="#eq:thermal72-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-coefficients">[eq:thermal72-coefficients]</a>, <a href="#eq:thermal72-horner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-horner">[eq:thermal72-horner]</a>, and <a href="#eq:thermal72-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-total">[eq:thermal72-total]</a> gives the following totals. The second column uses no twist; the third uses the $\pi$ twist.

<div class="center">

| $n$ |                          $W_{n,I}$ |                       $W_{n,P}$ |
|----:|-----------------------------------:|--------------------------------:|
|   4 | 1298092848946206529211955779616040 |  683496640248050713756440676071 |
|   5 |       9095379218684502088743241789 | 9205948219074287936685289758037 |
|   6 |   11971284263768914176431179720622 |  266573596119930013833919176645 |
|   7 |      15044150708248976451784593468 |  534330762492192297990602166370 |
|   8 |     386135473475360677201628357188 |   42459883501386986922766507327 |
|   9 |       4866417737994347551816846903 |   37716485750353424806876104310 |
|  10 |      19772960374215472827775892486 |    5030484777222468978572235564 |
|  11 |        841347687722481981930750335 |    2928767918985354021900689432 |
|  12 |       1266666171463993322695232916 |     524785463625387865619045258 |

</div>

We finish with a finite rational enclosure, specifying every bound used to turn these integers into the thermal table at $b=49/4$. Let $$r_{n,g}=\lfloor\sqrt{W_{n,g}}\rfloor,\qquad
 s_n=\sum_{j=0}^{100}\frac{x_n^j}{j!},\qquad
 t_n=\frac{x_n^{100}}{100!}.$$ Since $0\le x_n<15$, successive omitted terms have ratio at most $15/101$, and the tail is at most $15t_n/86<t_n$. Hence $s_n\le e^{x_n}\le s_n+t_n$. Equations <a href="#eq:thermal72-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-trace">[eq:thermal72-trace]</a> and <a href="#eq:thermal72-frobenius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:thermal72-frobenius">[eq:thermal72-frobenius]</a> give <a id="eq:thermal72-enclosure"></a>$$\label{eq:thermal72-enclosure}
 \begin{gathered}
 L_{n,g}=\frac{s_n\max(0,r_{n,g}-\mathcal E_n)^2}{Q^2},\qquad
 U_{n,g}=\frac{(s_n+t_n)(r_{n,g}+1+\mathcal E_n)^2}{Q^2},\\
 L_{n,g}\le Z_n(b,g)\le U_{n,g}.
 \end{gathered}$$ All quantities in these endpoints are rational or integer, with the integer square root determined by $r^2\le W<(r+1)^2$. Writing $C_{n,g}$ for the integer centers in the $b=49/4$ table of Lemma <a href="#lem:thermal-inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thermal-inputs">9</a>, direct integer and rational evaluation yields $$L_{n,g}>\frac{C_{n,g}-30}{10^6},
 \qquad
 U_{n,g}<\frac{C_{n,g}+30}{10^6}
 \qquad(4\le n\le12,\ g=I,P).$$ Every one of these strict inequalities has margin greater than $13/10^6$. The displayed algorithm, totals, and enclosures give a reproducible finite verification of all eighteen enclosures, and complete the proof of the thermal table at $b=49/4$.

<a id="sec:trials"></a>
### Two variational inputs

The finite initializations need a lower bound on the untwisted partition function at lengths $72$ and $120$. One fixed triple of integer matrices defines periodic matrix-product vectors at both lengths, with energy per bond below $-a=-700741/500000$. A block decomposition reduces the calculation to powers of integer matrices of order at most $162$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Norm and energy contractions

<a id="eq:trial-vector"></a>
<a id="eq:trial-transfer"></a>
<a id="eq:trial-insertion"></a>
For $N\ge4$ and three real $D$-by-$D$ matrices $A_s$, indexed by $s=-1,0,1$, define a vector in the standard orthonormal spin-one weight basis by $$\label{eq:trial-vector}
 \psi_N(s_0,\ldots,s_{N-1})
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(A_{s_0}\cdots A_{s_{N-1}}).$$ This is the periodic matrix-product representation of (Pérez-García et al. 2007, sec. 2.1, Equations (2)–(3)) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[PerezGarcia2007](#ref-PerezGarcia2007)]. Write $$\begin{aligned}
 B_{st}&=A_sA_t,\qquad T=\sum_{s=-1}^1 A_s\otimes A_s,
 \label{eq:trial-transfer}\\
 O&=\sum_{s,t=-1}^1 st\,B_{st}\otimes B_{st}
   +\sum_{\substack{s=-1,0\\t=0,1}}
    \bigl(B_{st}\otimes B_{s+1,t-1}
       +B_{s+1,t-1}\otimes B_{st}\bigr).
 \label{eq:trial-insertion}
\end{aligned}$$ All traces here are ordinary, unnormalized matrix traces.

<a id="stmt-lem-trial-contraction"></a>
<div id="lem:trial-contraction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *Let $N\ge4$ and $D\ge1$ be integers, and let $A_{-1},A_0,A_1\in M_D(\mathbb R)$. Define $\psi_N,T,O$ by Equations <a href="#eq:trial-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-vector">[eq:trial-vector]</a>–<a href="#eq:trial-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-insertion">[eq:trial-insertion]</a>. For the periodic spin-one Heisenberg Hamiltonian $H_N$, set $$V_N=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(T^N),\qquad U_N=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(T^{N-2}O).$$ Then $$\left\lVert \psi_N\right\rVert^2=V_N,\qquad
 \langle\psi_N,H_N\psi_N\rangle=NU_N.$$ In particular, if $V_N>0$ and $U_N<-aV_N$, then $E_0(N)<-Na$ and $Z_N(b)>1$ for every $b>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trial-contraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trial-contraction</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $T^N$ and use $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X\otimes Y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X)\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(Y)$. Since all amplitudes in <a href="#eq:trial-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-vector">[eq:trial-vector]</a> are real, the result is the sum of their squares. This proves the norm formula without assuming that the finite contraction matrix $T$ is self-adjoint or positive.

In the weight basis, a bond has diagonal entry $st$ on the pair $(s,t)$. Its off-diagonal entries are $1$ between $(s,t)$ and $(s+1,t-1)$ for $s=-1,0$ and $t=0,1$: each follows from $h=S^z\otimes S^z+(S^+\otimes S^-+S^-\otimes S^+)/2$ and the ladder entries $\sqrt2$. Inserting this bond into the two amplitude layers gives exactly $O$ in <a href="#eq:trial-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-insertion">[eq:trial-insertion]</a>. Summing the other physical indices therefore gives $U_N$ for one bond. Cyclicity of the amplitude trace makes $\psi_N$ invariant under cyclic site translation, so every bond, including the periodic seam, contributes equally.

For $V_N>0$, the variational principle gives $E_0(N)\le NU_N/V_N<-Na$. The shifted ground energy is negative, so its term in $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_N+NaI)}$ exceeds $1$ for every $b>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### One integer matrix triple

Set $D=26$. Index eight types by $\alpha=1,\ldots,8$, put $$(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_8)=(1,1,1,1,3,3,3,5),$$ and order the virtual labels $(\alpha,d)$ first by $\alpha$ and then by $d=-\ell_\alpha,-\ell_\alpha+2,\ldots,\ell_\alpha$. The matrices $A_s$ use the diagonal parameters $D_\alpha$ in Table <a href="#tab:trial-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:trial-diagonal">1</a> and the parameters $P_{\alpha\beta}$ in Table <a href="#tab:trial-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:trial-pairs">2</a>, defined only when $\ell_\beta=\ell_\alpha+2$.

<div id="tab:trial-diagonal">

|  $\alpha$  |      1 |     2 |    3 |   4 |     5 |   6 |   7 |     8 |
|:----------:|-------:|------:|-----:|----:|------:|----:|----:|------:|
| $D_\alpha$ | $-289$ | $-84$ | $-9$ |  33 | $-22$ |  27 |  71 | $-11$ |

Diagonal parameters, common to both lengths.

</div>

<div id="tab:trial-pairs">

| $(\alpha,\beta)$ | $P_{\alpha\beta}$ | $(\alpha,\beta)$ | $P_{\alpha\beta}$ | $(\alpha,\beta)$ | $P_{\alpha\beta}$ |
|:----------------:|------------------:|:----------------:|------------------:|:----------------:|------------------:|
|     $(1,5)$      |             $-11$ |     $(2,7)$      |             $-49$ |     $(4,6)$      |                 9 |
|     $(1,6)$      |             $-35$ |     $(3,5)$      |              $-6$ |     $(4,7)$      |             $-32$ |
|     $(1,7)$      |             $-28$ |     $(3,6)$      |             $-25$ |     $(5,8)$      |                 1 |
|     $(2,5)$      |             $-23$ |     $(3,7)$      |             $-20$ |     $(6,8)$      |                 2 |
|     $(2,6)$      |                16 |     $(4,5)$      |             $-34$ |     $(7,8)$      |             $-16$ |

Parameters joining virtual types whose labels $\ell$ differ by $2$.

</div>

<a id="eq:trial-support"></a>
<a id="eq:trial-entry"></a>
Here are complete entry rules. Consider the row $(\alpha,d)$ and column $(\beta,e)$, put $\ell=\ell_\alpha$, $k=\ell_\beta$, and fix $s\in\{-1,0,1\}$. The entry is zero unless $$\label{eq:trial-support}
 e=d+2s,\qquad |k-\ell|\le2,\qquad
 (k=\ell\Longrightarrow\alpha=\beta).$$ For an allowed entry define $$p=\begin{cases}
 D_\alpha,& k=\ell,\\
 P_{\alpha\beta},& k=\ell+2,\\
 -P_{\beta\alpha},& k=\ell-2.
 \end{cases}$$ Put $r=(d+\ell)/2$ and set the entry equal to $p f$, where $$\label{eq:trial-entry}
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 &s=-1&s=0&s=1\\ \hline
k=\ell+2&
 2(\ell+1-r)(\ell+2-r)&2(r+1)(\ell-r+1)&(r+1)(r+2)\\
k=\ell&
 2(\ell-r+1)&d&-(r+1)\\
k=\ell-2&2&-2&1
\end{array}$$ lists the values of $f$. These entry rules are independent of $N$.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Finite contractions and variational bounds

We now evaluate the contractions in Lemma <a href="#lem:trial-contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trial-contraction">16</a> for this fixed triple at $N=72$ and $N=120$. The support rule <a href="#eq:trial-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-support">[eq:trial-support]</a> makes this a short block calculation. On paired virtual labels $((\alpha,d),(\beta,e))$, both $T$ and $O$ preserve $d-e$. For $T$, the two layers shift by the same $2s$. For $O$, the two layers have the same total physical weight $s+t$, also in each off-diagonal term. Thus the difference blocks are exhaustive and have dimensions <a id="eq:trial-blocks"></a>$$\label{eq:trial-blocks}
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrrrrrrr}
d-e&-10&-8&-6&-4&-2&0&2&4&6&8&10\\ \hline
\text{dimension}&1&8&32&80&136&162&136&80&32&8&1
\end{array}.$$ They sum to $676=26^2$; each block contributes once.

For completeness, the entire integer computation consists of the following operations. Form $A_s$, $B_{st}$, $T$, and $O$ from Tables <a href="#tab:trial-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:trial-diagonal">1</a>–<a href="#tab:trial-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:trial-pairs">2</a> and Equations <a href="#eq:trial-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-transfer">[eq:trial-transfer]</a>, <a href="#eq:trial-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-insertion">[eq:trial-insertion]</a>, <a href="#eq:trial-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-support">[eq:trial-support]</a>, and <a href="#eq:trial-entry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-entry">[eq:trial-entry]</a>. Restrict $T,O$ to each difference block, obtaining $T_\delta,O_\delta$. Compute powers by $$P_0=I,\qquad
 P_{2j}=P_j^2\quad(j\ge1),\qquad
 P_{2j+1}=P_j^2T_\delta\quad(j\ge0).$$ Then accumulate <a id="eq:trial-integer-algorithm"></a>$$\label{eq:trial-integer-algorithm}
 U_N=\sum_\delta\sum_{i,j}
       (P_{N-2})_{ij}(O_\delta)_{ji},
 \qquad
 V_N=\sum_\delta\sum_{i,j}
       (P_{N-2})_{ij}(T_\delta^2)_{ji}.$$ Only arbitrary-precision integer operations are used. For $N=72$, the required power is $70$. For $N=120$, one may use $T^{118}=T^{64}T^{32}T^{16}T^4T^2$ and $T^{120}=T^{64}T^{32}T^{16}T^8$ within each block.

<a id="eq:trial-integer-results"></a>
The resulting exact integer comparisons are $$\label{eq:trial-integer-results}
 V_N>0,\qquad
 -c_NV_N\le10^{12}U_N<(-c_N+1)V_N,
 \qquad
 \begin{array}{c|r}
 N&c_N\\ \hline
 72&1401482112601\\
 120&1401482105174
 \end{array}.$$ The supplied computation files contain the complete matrices and the full integers $U_N,V_N$, rather than only these shortened intervals. In particular, the files and record the full contractions and positive margins $-700741V_N-500000U_N$. The common matrices are recorded in . The program , with `--length 72` or `--length 120`, executes the entry rules and block contractions in at the selected length. The integer comparisons in <a href="#eq:trial-integer-results" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-integer-results">[eq:trial-integer-results]</a> also follow directly from their full recorded integers.

<a id="stmt-lem-trial-inputs"></a>
<div id="lem:trial-inputs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *For the untwisted periodic spin-one Heisenberg Hamiltonian and $a=700741/500000$, $$E_0(72)<-72a,\qquad E_0(120)<-120a.$$ Consequently $Z_{72}(b)>1$ and $Z_{120}(b)>1$ for every $b>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trial-inputs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trial-inputs</summary>

*Proof.* For each row of <a href="#eq:trial-integer-results" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trial-integer-results">[eq:trial-integer-results]</a>, $(-c_N+1)/10^{12}<-700741/500000$. Thus the corresponding explicit matrices have $V_N>0$ and $U_N/V_N<-a$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:trial-contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trial-contraction">16</a>. ◻

</details>

The support rule puts these trial vectors in total weight zero, but the variational bound is for the minimum of the full physical spectrum. Their only symmetry used in the contraction proof is cyclic site translation. The trial inputs hold at every positive inverse temperature, so each finite initialization can use them at its own temperature.

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\draw[->] (-0.4,-0.55) -- (7.2,-0.55) node[right] {$L$};\n \\draw[->] (-0.4,-0.55) -- (-0.4,2.65) node[above] {$b$};\n \\foreach \\x/\\lab in {0/n_0,2.2/2n_0,4.4/4n_0,6.6/8n_0}\n   {\\draw (\\x,-0.5)--(\\x,-0.6);\n    \\node[below] at (\\x,-0.6) {$\\lab$};}\n \\foreach \\y/\\lab in {0/b_0,1/2b_0,2/4b_0}\n   {\\node[left] at (-0.4,\\y) {$\\lab$};}\n \\draw[very thick] (0,0)--(2.2,0);\n \\draw[very thick] (2.2,1)--(4.4,1);\n \\draw[very thick] (4.4,2)--(6.6,2);\n \\fill (0,0) circle (1.6pt);\n \\fill (2.2,1) circle (1.6pt);\n \\fill (4.4,2) circle (1.6pt);\n \\draw[->,dashed] (0.12,0.08)--(2.08,0.92);\n \\draw[->,dashed] (2.32,1.08)--(4.28,1.92);\n \\node[below] at (1.1,0) {interpolate};\n \\node[below] at (3.3,1) {interpolate};\n \\node[below] at (5.5,2) {interpolate};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D diagram with horizontal axis $L$ (arrow pointing right) and vertical axis $b$ (arrow pointing up). Horizontal axis has tick marks labeled $n_0$, $2n_0$, $4n_0$, $8n_0$; vertical axis has tick marks labeled $b_0$, $2b_0$, $4b_0$. At each temperature level $b_0$, $2b_0$, and $4b_0$, a solid horizontal interval is shown with the text \"interpolate\" beneath it. Dashed arrows point diagonally upward and to the right, doubling the starting length and inverse temperature between successive scales."
    }
